   - Where the compromise appears to have started without a clear installed-app source, consider 0-click media decode, browser renderer, or other pre-installation surfaces.
   - Project Zero writeups are the methodological reference here, not a checklist of known bugs.

5. **Kernel/privilege escalation reconstruction**
   - Match device state, kernel version, patch level, and crash/tombstone evidence against CVE and vendor advisory data.
   - Use kernel exploit resources for understanding plausibility and class of primitive, not for assuming a specific bug without evidence.

6. **Attribution caveats**
   - A full chain may leave very little clean forensic residue, especially if the exploit and post-exploitation are designed to be quiet.
   - Attribution to a specific actor usually needs more than device artifacts: campaign context, targeting patterns, infrastructure, and often external intelligence.
   - Treat attribution as a hypothesis to be corroborated, not a conclusion from a single device snapshot.

## 6. Bottom line

A full Android exploit chain is usually a stack of primitives:

- App-layer or 0-click/browser entry.
- Sandbox or IPC breakout.
- Privilege escalation to a powerful context.
- Post-exploitation tooling such as Ghost.

drozer is the app-layer IPC assessment tool that helps find and understand the app-layer footholds. Ghost is the post-exploitation rider that becomes relevant after access is gained. The most useful forensic posture is to work backward from post-exploitation artifacts and device state through app-layer and browser/0-click possibilities to the most plausible entry and escalation primitives, while being explicit about what the evidence supports and what it does not.

```

`android-exploit-identification.md`:

```md
================================================================================
ANDROID EXPLOIT IDENTIFICATION & KERNEL EXPLOIT REPRO RESOURCES
================================================================================
Compiled: 2026-09-04
Sources: GitHub, Google Project Zero, Android Offensive Security Blog,
         ReversecLabs, EntySec, cloudfuzz.github.io, xairy/linux-kernel-exploitation

================================================================================
SECTION 1: MOST USEFUL TOOLS / REPOS — SHORT LIST
================================================================================

1. ashishb/android-security-awesome (9.7k stars)
   - Master index: Tools, Exploits/Vulnerabilities/Bugs, Academic papers.
   - Why it matters: Single best starting point; curated taxonomy of Android
     security resources. Use it to orient before diving into any sub-topic.

2. xairy/linux-kernel-exploitation (6.6k stars, updated Aug 2026)
   - Curated link index: books, techniques, vulnerabilities (LPE/RCE/KVM),
     exploit mitigations, fuzzers, CTF tasks, practice environments.
   - Why it matters: The de facto general Linux kernel exploitation reference.
     Cross-references many Android-specific CVEs (CVE-2019-2215, CVE-2022-20409
     io_uring, CVE-2022-0847 Dirty Pipe, etc.). Bimonthly updates — stays live.

3. ReversecLabs/drozer (4.6k stars)
   - Android IPC security assessment framework: runs as an app, enumerates
     exported components (activities, services, content providers, broadcast
     receivers), port 31415, direct + infrastructure mode, includes Sieve
     vulnerable app for practice.
   - Why it matters: Primary tool for app-layer IPC surface discovery. Not a
     kernel exploit tool, but essential pre-exploit recon — tells you what an
     attacker-controlled app can reach.

4. EntySec/Ghost (3.4k stars)
   - Android post-exploitation RAT via ADB. Python-based, pip-installable.
   - Why it matters: Demonstrates what an attacker does AFTER gaining ADB access
     (or after a kernel exploit that gives shell). Command execution, file
     operations, shell upgrades. Relevant for the "post-exploit" phase of an
     isolate-and-identify workflow.

5. sfewer-r7/pocindex (82k PoCs)
   - Aggregated PoC index across many CVEs.
   - Why it matters: Largest single PoC aggregation point. Use to find existing
     exploit code for a CVE you've matched to a device.

6. 0xbinder/android-kernel-exploitation-lab (43 stars, active Apr 2025)
   - Hands-on lab for CVE-2019-2215 (Binder UAF). Includes QEMU setup, kernel
     build configs, exploit code, GDB scripts, fuzzing guides (syzkaller, AFL++).
   - Why it matters: Most directly usable Android kernel exploitation walkthrough
     available. Reproduces a real Android kernel CVE end-to-end.

7. sharif-dev/AndroidKernelVulnerability
   - Triggering and analyzing CVE-2017-11176 (kernel UAF).
   - Why it matters: Older CVE but shows methodology: trigger, analyze, exploit.

8. mito753/Kernel-Exploit-Dojo
   - CTF-style kernel pwn challenges with writeups. Covers core_pattern race,
     seccomp backdoors, io_uring bypasses, cross-cache UAF, modprobe_path.
   - Why it matters: Practical kernel exploit technique reference — techniques
     that transfer to Android kernel exploits.

9. Google Project Zero blog — "Bad Binder" (CVE-2019-2215), "Analyzing a Modern
   In-the-wild Android Exploit" (CVE-2023-0266 ALSA + CVE-2023-26083 Mali)
   - Why it matters: Authoritative technical writeups of real in-the-wild exploit
     chains. Shows attacker methodology: bug selection, heap spray, KASLR defeat,
     arbitrary r/w primitive construction.

10. cloudfuzz.github.io/android-kernel-exploitation
    - Structured educational site on Android kernel exploitation (CVE-2019-2215).
    - Why it matters: Accessible walkthrough of vulnerability discovery → patch
      analysis → exploitation for a real Android kernel CVE.

================================================================================
SECTION 2: THE SAELO REPO — WHAT REPLACED IT
================================================================================

The saelo/Android-kernel-exploitation or saelo/wtf repo (the well-known Android
kernel exploitation resource) appears to have been removed or made private. As of
this writing there is no exact 1:1 replacement publicly live under that name.

In practice, the field has fragmented into several active resources that together
cover the same ground:

A. xairy/linux-kernel-exploitation
   - The closest general replacement for the "kernel exploitation knowledge base"
     function. It is actively maintained (last commit Aug 2026), covers Android-
     relevant CVEs, and links to exploit code and writeups.

B. 0xbinder/android-kernel-exploitation-lab
   - The closest functional replacement for "here is a walkthrough of exploiting
     an Android kernel CVE." Focused on CVE-2019-2215 with full lab setup.

C. Google Android Offensive Security Blog (androidoffsec.withgoogle.com)
   - Official Google resource. Posts like "Attacking Android Binder: Analysis and
     Exploitation of CVE-2023-20938" (June 2024) are the modern equivalent of
     what saelo's material taught: real Android kernel bug → exploit primitive →
     root.

D. Google Project Zero blog
   - In-the-wild Android exploit analyses. The definitive source for how Android
     kernel exploits actually work in the wild, not just in CTFs.

E. Kernel-Exploit-Dojo (mito753) + r1ru/linux-kernel-exploitation
   - For technique-level learning: cross-cache attacks, UAF reclaim, ROP, modprobe
     _path overwrites — all transferable to Android.

Bottom line: there is no single repo that has fully absorbed the saelo resource.
The active "kernel exploitation on Android" space is now served by:
   - xairy/linux-kernel-exploitation (index)
   - 0xbinder/android-kernel-exploitation-lab (hands-on CVE walkthrough)
   - Android OffSec blog + Project Zero (real-world analysis)
   - Individual CVE PoC repos linked from pocindex

================================================================================
SECTION 3: STEP-BY-STEP ISOLATE-AND-IDENTIFY WORKFLOW
================================================================================

GOAL: From suspicious behavior on a device → concrete CVE/kernel version match →
PoC correlation → attribution.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 0: DETECT & TRIAGE (suspicious behavior observed)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Signs that warrant escalation:
- Unexpected root shell or su access
- ADB daemon (adbd) running in insecure mode (ro.debuggable=1, adbd on network)
- Device overheating, battery drain, network spikes with no user cause
- Unknown apps in package manager, especially with SYSTEM or root UID
- SELinux status: getenforce returning "Permissive" or "Disabled" on a device
  that should be Enforcing
- Kernel panic / tombstone logs referencing suspicious addresses
- Network connections to unexpected IPs/ports from system services
- Logcat showing repeated crashes in security-critical components (mediaserver,
  keystore, binder, surfaceflinger)

Immediate triage commands (requires adb shell, ideally root):
  getenforce
  adb shell getprop ro.debuggable
  adb shell ps -A | grep -iE 'root|system|adbd|shell'
  adb shell netstat -tulpn  (or ss if available)
  adb shell logcat -d | tail -1000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 1: ISOLATE THE DEVICE
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Goal: Prevent further compromise, preserve evidence, maintain ADB/debug access.

1.1 Physical/network isolation
  - Disable Wi-Fi, mobile data, Bluetooth from settings or adb shell cmd wifi
  - If ADB over TCP is enabled (adb tcpip), switch back to USB mode:
      adb shell setprop service.adb.tcp.port -1
  - Do NOT power off the device — volatile memory and running processes are
    evidence. Keep it charged and connected.

1.2 Preserve ADB access
  - If adbd is running and you have ADB keys, keep the connection open.
  - Take screenshots of the device UI with adb shell screencap.
  - If possible, enable verbose logging before anything else:
      adb shell setprop persist.log.tag *:V
      (note: requires appropriate privileges; may not work on production builds)

1.3 If adbd is NOT available and device is locked
  - Document what is visible (photos of screen, physical inspection).
  - Do not attempt a factory reset — that destroys evidence.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 2: ARTIFACT COLLECTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Collect BEFORE any analysis that might alter state. Work from a copy, not the
device directly where possible.

2.1 logcat (system + crash logs)
  adb logcat -d -v time -b all > logcat_full.txt
  adb logcat -d -b crash > logcat_crash.txt
  adb logcat -d -b system > logcat_system.txt
  adb logcat -d -b main   > logcat_main.txt

  Key things to grep for:
    - "Fatal", "SIGSEGV", "SIGABRT", "Tombstone"
    - "CVE" or vulnerability-related strings (rare but possible in debug builds)
    - "binder", "io_uring", "ashmem", "mali", "adreno", "kgsl" (kernel subsystem
      hints)
    - Permission denial: "java.lang.SecurityException", "Permission Denial"
    - SELinux denials: "avc:  denied"
    - Unexpected UID transitions, package installations
    - ANR (Application Not Responding) traces

2.2 dumpsys (service state)
  adb shell dumpsys > dumpsys_full.txt       (entire system services dump)
  adb shell dumpsys package > dumpsys_package.txt
  adb shell dumpsys activity > dumpsys_activity.txt
  adb shell dumpsys meminfo > dumpsys_meminfo.txt
  adb shell dumpsys netstat > dumpsys_netstat.txt
  adb shell dumpsys battery > dumpsys_battery.txt
  adb shell dumpsys deviceidle > dumpsys_deviceidle.txt

  Key packages to inspect in dumpsys package:
    - Look for apps with android:sharedUserId="android.uid.system" (00000.0)
    - Look for apps with debuggable="true"
    - Look for unknown packages, especially with high privilege
    - Check permission trees and granted permissions per package

2.3 Package & permission inspection
  adb shell pm list packages -f > pm_packages_f.txt
  adb shell pm list packages -3 > pm_third_party.txt   (user-installed)
  adb shell pm list packages -s > pm_system.txt        (system)
  adb shell pm list packages -e > pm_enabled.txt
  adb shell pm list packages -d > pm_disabled.txt

  For each suspicious package:
    adb shell dumpsys package <pkgname> > pkg_<name>.txt
    adb shell pm path <pkgname>                           (APK location)

  Permission audit:
    adb shell dumpsys package <pkgname> | grep -A50 "requested permissions"
    adb shell dumpsys package <pkgname> | grep -A20 "granted permissions"

2.4 APK hashes & extraction
  For each suspicious or unknown APK:
    adb pull /data/app/<path>/base.apk   (or wherever pm path reports)
    sha256sum <apk_file>
    sha1sum <apk_file>
    md5sum <apk_file>

  Store hashes in a structured record:
    - APK filename
    - Package name
    - Install time (from dumpsys package — "codePath" timestamps or first install
      time)
    - Signature (apksigner verify --print-certs, or jarsigner -verify -verbose)

  Additional APK metadata:
    aapt dump badging <apk>          (permissions, activities, SDK versions)
    aapt dump permissions <apk>
    unzip -l <apk> | grep -E '\.so$'  (native libraries — hints at exploit code)

2.5 Kernel version / build fingerprint
  adb shell getprop ro.build.fingerprint
  adb shell getprop ro.build.display.id
  adb shell getprop ro.kernel.version      (if available)
  adb shell cat /proc/version
  adb shell uname -a

  The build fingerprint is critical — it encodes device model, Android version,
  build date, and often the kernel variant. Example:
    'Samsung/GT-I9505/Instrumented/Android 9/XXUENKF7/..."
  This maps to a specific security patch level via the Android Security Bulletin.

  Patch level:
    adb shell getprop ro.build.version.security_patch
    adb shell getprop ro.build.version.release

  Kernel config (if rooted):
    adb shell zcat /proc/config.gz   > kernel_config.gz   (if CONFIG_IKCONFIG=y)
    adb shell cat /proc/sys/kernel/perf_event_paranoid
    adb shell cat /sys/devices/system/cpu/smt/active

2.6 Native crash / tombstone collection
  adb pull /data/tombstones/  ./tombstones/
  adb shell ls -la /data/tombstones/

  Also check:
    adb shell ls -la /data/system/dropbox/   (system dropbox logs)
    adb shell logcat -d -b crash             (crash buffer often has tombstone
                                                content inline)

  Tombstones are especially valuable for kernel exploits — they may show the
  faulting PC, register state, and backtrace at the moment of a kernel panic
  triggered by a malformed exploit attempt.

2.7 Running process snapshot
  adb shell ps -A -o pid,uid,comm,args > ps_snapshot.txt
  adb shell cat /proc/<pid>/maps        for suspicious PIDs
  adb shell cat /proc/<pid>/status     for UIDs, capabilities

2.8 Network state snapshot
  adb shell netstat -tulpn > netstat.txt    (or ss -tulpn)
  adb shell cat /proc/net/tcp
  adb shell cat /proc/net/tcp6
  adb shell dumpsys connectivity

2.9 SELinux state
  adb shell getenforce
  adb shell dmesg | grep -i avc | tail -100    > selinux_avc_denials.txt
  (or adb shell logcat -d | grep "avc:  denied")

2.10 ADB configuration state
  adb shell getprop service.adb.tcp.port
  adb shell getprop ro.debuggable
  adb shell ls -la /dev/usb/laptopmode
  adb shell settings list global | grep -i adb
  adb shell settings list secure | grep -i adb

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 3: CVE / KERNEL VERSION MATCHING
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

3.1 Establish the target profile
  From Phase 2 you now have:
    - Device model (from fingerprint)
    - Android version (ro.build.version.release)
    - Security patch level (ro.build.version.security_patch)
    - Kernel version (uname -a / proc/version)
    - OEM (Samsung, Pixel, Xiaomi, etc. — determines patch backport behavior)

3.2 Map to vulnerable window
  The Android Security Bulletins are the authoritative source. Key URL pattern:
    https://source.android.com/docs/security/bulletin/<year>-<month>-01

  For each month, the bulletin lists:
    - CVE ID
    - Affected versions (Android version + device-specific)
    - Severity
    - Kernel component (binder, gpu, alsa, netfilter, etc.)
    - Patch status

  Cross-reference your device's security patch level:
    - If device patch level is 2023-01-05, it is vulnerable to CVEs assigned
      before that date AND not backported.
    - If device patch level is 2023-02-01 or later, CVE-2023-20938 (Binder UAF)
      should be patched (though see partial-fix caveat in the OffSec blog — the
      Feb 2023 patch was incomplete; full fix was July 2023 bulletin).

  Important caveat: Android OEMs backport on different schedules. A Pixel device
  may be patched for a CVE that a Samsung device with the same stated patch level
  is not. Always check OEM-specific bulletins when available.

3.3 Kernel version → upstream CVE mapping
  Use the kernel version string to identify the upstream Linux kernel base, then
  search:
    - xairy/linux-kernel-exploitation README → "Vulnerabilities" → "LPE" or "RCE"
    - NVD: https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/search
    - Kernel git log for the specific subsystem (e.g., drivers/android/binder.c
      commit history)

  Known Android-relevant kernel CVEs (partial list):
    CVE-2019-2215  Binder UAF (epoll + BINDER_THREAD_EXIT)
    CVE-2020-0041  Binder OOB access (sandbox escape)
    CVE-2020-0423  Binder race (Typhoon Mangkhut)
    CVE-2022-20409 io_uring (LPE)
    CVE-2022-0847  Dirty Pipe (pipe buffer flag)
    CVE-2022-20421 Bad Spin (spinlock UAF)
    CVE-2023-0266  ALSA compat layer race (in-the-wild, Samsung)
    CVE-2023-20938 Binder UAF (GKI 5.4/5.10, partial fix Feb 2023, full fix Jul 2023
    CVE-2023-26083 Mali tlstream info leak (in-the-wild, Samsung)
    CVE-2023-21255 Binder UAF (full fix for CVE-2023-20938, Jul 2023)
    CVE-2024-0582  (GPU/driver — recent)
    CVE-2025-21479 Adreno (GPU driver)
    CVE-2025-48595 Framework privilege escalation (actively exploited, Jun 2026
      bulletin — Android 14/15/16 affected; 124 vulnerabilities patched total;
      confirmed "under limited, targeted exploitation" per Google)
    CVE-2026-31431 Copy Fail (recent kernel copy primitive)
    CVE-2025-68260 rust_binder (first Rust-code CVE in Linux kernel)

3.4 Component hints from artifacts
  Use the artifact review to narrow the search:
    - logcat mentions "binder" + crash → binder CVEs
    - logcat mentions "mali", "kgsl", "adreno", "gpu" → GPU driver CVEs
    - logcat mentions "alsa", "snd" → ALSA CVEs
    - logcat mentions "ashmem", "ion", "dmabuf" → memory subsystem CVEs
    - Tombstone with kernel PC in drivers/android/ → binder/kernel Android driver
    - Process maps showing GPU libraries (libGLES_mali, libadreno, etc.) → GPU CVEs

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 4: PoC CORRELATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

4.1 Search PoC repositories
  Primary: sfewer-r7/pocindex (82k PoCs) — search by CVE ID.
  Secondary: GitHub search by CVE ID, e.g.:
    site:github.com "CVE-2019-2215" exploit
    site:github.com "CVE-2022-0847" dirtypipe

  Also check Exploit-DB:
    https://www.exploit-db.com/search?q=CVE-2019-2215

4.2 Evaluate the PoC
  For each candidate PoC, assess:
    a) Does it target the same kernel version / subsystem as the device?
    b) Does it require the same access level the attacker had? (untrusted app,
       system_server, ADB shell, root?)
    c) Does it match the observed behavior? (e.g., if you saw a Binder-related
       crash, a Binder UAF PoC is more likely than an io_uring PoC)
    d) Is the PoC for the upstream kernel or the Android-specific variant? Some
       Android kernel CVEs have different triggering paths than upstream.

  Specific PoC repositories of note:
    - CVE-2019-2215: Exploit-DB #47463, 0xbinder/android-kernel-exploitation-lab
      exploit-code/, Project Zero issue #1942 comment #7
    - CVE-2022-0847 Dirty Pipe: haxx.in/files/dirtypipez.c, multiple GitHub repos
    - CVE-2022-20409 io_uring: ysanatomic/io_uring_LPE-CVE-2023-2598 (adjacent)
    - CVE-2023-0266 + CVE-2023-26083: full chain analysis in Project Zero blog;
      no public standalone PoC (in-the-wild, 0-day at discovery)
    - CVE-2023-20938: analysis in Android OffSec blog; no standalone public PoC
      at time of writing (Google internal exploit used in research)

4.3 Reproduce in a lab environment (if feasible)
  For educational/recon purposes, reproduce in an emulator or spare device:
    - Use 0xbinder/android-kernel-exploitation-lab for CVE-2019-2215 (QEMU-based)
    - Build the specific kernel version from Android Common Kernel source:
        https://source.android.com/docs/core/architecture/kernel/downloading
    - Use the custom-manifest from 0xbinder lab to set up the correct AOSP +
      kernel combo

  Do NOT test PoCs against the compromised device itself — that destroys evidence
  and may trigger additional exploitation.

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 5: ATTRIBUTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

5.1 What attribution means here
  Attribution in Android exploit cases usually means:
    - Identifying which CVE(s) were used
    - Determining the exploit chain structure (e.g., browser RCE → sandbox escape
      → kernel LPE → root)
    - Identifying the likely threat actor (if in-the-wild) based on tooling,
      targets, and TTPs
    - Determining whether the exploit used n-days (known, unpatched in downstream)
      or 0-days

5.2 Evidence sources for attribution
  - The specific CVE(s) matched (some CVEs are associated with specific campaigns
    in Project Zero / TAG writeups)
  - Exploit technique fingerprints: heap spray method, KASLR defeat technique,
    arbitrary r/w primitive construction — these can be characteristic of a
    specific exploit developer or group
  - Timestamps: when the exploit ran (logcat timestamps), correlated with when the
    device was vulnerable (patch level vs. CVE disclosure date)
  - Package/APK artifacts: if the exploit was delivered via an app, the APK itself
    (hash, signature, code similarities) can be compared to known samples
  - Network indicators: C2 IPs, domains, protocols from netstat / packet capture

5.3 Reference attribution sources
  - Google TAG blog posts (blog.google/threat-analysis-group) — attribute exploit
    chains to specific spyware vendors
  - Project Zero in-the-wild blog posts — technical attribution of exploit
    techniques
  - Citizen Lab / Amnesty International Security Lab reports — campaign-level
    attribution for mobile exploit chains
  - Android Security Bulletins — indicate which CVEs were exploited in the wild

5.4 Limitations
  - Many Android kernel exploits leave minimal forensic traces if they succeed
    cleanly (no crash, no tombstone).
  - Post-exploitation tools like Ghost may obscure the original entry vector.
  - If SELinux was already Permissive, the device may have been compromised before
    the kernel exploit stage.
  - Attribution to a specific actor without additional intelligence (network,
    malware samples, endpoint telemetry) is generally not possible from device
    artifacts alone.

================================================================================
SECTION 4: DROPZER'S EXPLOIT MODULES AND GHOST FRAMEWORK — HOW THEY FIT
================================================================================

4.1 drozer (ReversecLabs/drozer)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

WHAT IT IS:
  Android IPC security assessment framework. Runs as an installed agent app on
  the target device (drozer-agent.apk). A client console (Python) connects to the
  agent via port 31415 (direct mode) or through an infrastructure proxy. The
  framework ships with a vulnerable sample app (Sieve) for learning.

WHAT IT DOES (exploit-relevant):
  - Enumerates exported IPC components: activities, services, content providers,
    broadcast receivers — `run app.package.attacksurface <package>`
  - Retrieves package metadata: UID, GID, permissions, data directory, APK path —
    `run app.package.info -a <package>`
  - Tests content provider SQL injection, path traversal, URI manipulation
  - Tests exported service IPC for command injection or insecure Binder
    interfaces
  - Can interact with Android system services via Binder from the app context
  - Custom module support: Python-based modules can be written to automate any
    IPC-level test

WHAT IT DOES NOT DO:
  - It does not exploit kernel vulnerabilities directly.
  - It does not escalate from app sandbox to root by itself.
  - It does not achieve code execution outside the app's own SELinux context.

HOW IT FITS IN THE WORKFLOW:
  drozer is a Phase 2/Phase 3 tool — it helps you understand what an attacker
  controlling an app (or an app-like entity) can reach BEFORE any kernel exploit.
  Use it to:
    (a) Discover if there is an app-layer vulnerability that gives a higher
        privilege level (e.g., an exported content provider that leaks sensitive
        data, an exported service with a command injection).
    (b) Map the IPC attack surface as part of artifact collection — the exported
        components list is itself evidence of the device's exposure.
    (c) Test whether a suspicious app exposes dangerous IPC endpoints that could
        be the entry point for an exploit chain.

EXPLOIT MODULE CONTEXT:
  drozer's modules are primarily assessment/test modules, not weaponized exploits.
  There are modules for content provider testing, permission auditing, and
  component interaction. If a module "exploits" something, it does so within the
  app sandbox — e.g., extracting data from an insecure content provider. For
  kernel-level exploitation, drozer's role is reconnaissance, not the exploit
  itself.

4.2 Ghost Framework (EntySec/Ghost)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

WHAT IT IS:
  Android post-exploitation framework that operates over ADB. Python-based,
  pip-installable (pip3 install git+https://github.com/EntySec/Ghost). Assumes
  you already have ADB access to the target device — either USB ADB with appropriate
  auth, or ADB over TCP.

WHAT IT DOES:
  - Command execution on the device via ADB shell
  - File upload/download
  - Shell upgrade (interactive shell via ADB)
  - Device enumeration: installed apps, running processes, network info
  - APK extraction, package management
  - Essentially a RAT (Remote Access Trojan) interface for ADB-connected devices

PREREQUISITE:
  Ghost requires ADB access. It does not gain ADB access by itself. ADB access
  can come from:
    - USB debugging enabled + authorized RSA key (physical access or previously
      trusted)
    - ADB over TCP (adbd listening on a network port) — this is a common
      misconfiguration on older/embedded Android devices, and also the vector for
      CVE-2024-0043 / related ADB authentication bypasses
    - A kernel exploit that escalates to root and enables ADB (some exploits do
      this as a post-exploit step)

HOW IT FITS IN THE WORKFLOW:
  Ghost is a Phase 5 tool — it represents what an attacker does AFTER gaining
  shell access. In an isolate-and-identify context:
    (a) If you find evidence that Ghost (or similar ADB-based tooling) is running
        on the device, that is a strong indicator of post-exploitation activity.
        Look for: adb shell processes with Ghost-related commands, suspicious
        Python processes, network connections to Ghost C2 if applicable.
    (b) Ghost's capabilities help you understand the attacker's foothold: if they
        had ADB shell, they could pull APKs, read logcat persistently, inspect
        memory, extract keys from keystore (if rooted), and install additional
        tools.
    (c) Ghost is relevant to the "attribution" phase — ADB-based post-exploitation
        frameworks are commonly used by both security testers and threat actors.
        The presence of Ghost specifically (vs. a custom script) may indicate a
        less sophisticated actor or a repurposed open-source tool.

LIMITATIONS:
  - Ghost does not exploit kernel vulnerabilities. It operates entirely within the
    ADB shell context.
  - If the device is not rooted, ADB shell is limited to the shell UID (2000) —
    many sensitive operations (reading /data/user/0/<app>/files, accessing
    keystore, modifying SELinux) require root.
  - Modern Android versions with ADB auth and USB debugging restrictions limit
    Ghost's reach significantly compared to older devices.

4.3 How They Work Together in an Exploit Chain
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

A typical full-chain exploit scenario:

  1. Initial access: attacker delivers a malicious app, or exploits a browser
     renderer vulnerability (e.g., CVE-2022-4262 Chrome 0-day in the Samsung
     browser, per Project Zero TAG analysis).

  2. Sandbox escape / privilege escalation: the attacker uses an app-layer
     vulnerability (exported IPC component, content provider injection) or a
     kernel CVE (Binder UAF, GPU driver bug) to escape the app sandbox and gain
     system_server or root privileges.

     → drozer's role: if you're analyzing this chain forensically, drozer-style
       IPC enumeration of the malicious app reveals what app-layer surface it
       exposed or exploited. drozer can replicate the IPC-level access to confirm
       the vulnerability.

  3. Kernel exploit (if needed): if the attacker needs kernel primitives (arbitrary
     read/write, KASLR defeat), they trigger a kernel CVE. This may leave a
     tombstone, a kernel panic, or subtle logcat artifacts.

     → drozer's role: none directly. drozer doesn't touch the kernel.
     → 0xbinder lab / xairy index / Project Zero blog: these are the resources for
       understanding what kernel CVE was likely used.

  4. Post-exploitation: with root or ADB shell, the attacker uses a framework like
     Ghost to maintain access, exfiltrate data, install persistence, etc.

     → Ghost's role: if you detect Ghost or similar tooling on the device, it
       confirms post-exploitation activity. The Ghost command history (if recoverable
       from shell history or process memory) tells you what the attacker did.

  5. Forensice isolate-and-identify: you, the investigator, go backwards through
     this chain — starting from post-exploit artifacts (Ghose process, extracted
     APKs), through kernel artifacts (tombstones, logcat), through app-layer
     artifacts (exported components, suspicious packages), to identify the entry
     point and the CVEs used.

================================================================================
SECTION 5: SUMMARY — QUICK REFERENCE CARD
================================================================================

TOOLS:
  ashishb/android-security-awesome   — Master index, start here
  xairy/linux-kernel-exploitation    — Kernel exploit link index (active, Aug 2026)
  0xbinder/android-kernel-exploitation-lab — CVE-2019-2215 hands-on lab
  ReversecLabs/drozer                — IPC surface enumeration, app-layer recon
  EntySec/Ghost                       — ADB post-exploitation RAT
  sfewer-r7/pocindex                 — PoC aggregation (82k)
  Project Zero blog                  — In-the-wild Android exploit analysis
  Android OffSec blog                — Google's Binder/kernel exploit writeups

SAELO REPLACEMENT:
  No single repo. Active landscape = xairy index + 0xbinder lab + Google OffSec
  blog + Project Zero + individual CVE PoC repos.

WORKFLOW (5 phases):
  Phase 0: Detect & triage  — logcat, getenforce, ps, netstat
  Phase 1: Isolate           — network off, ADB preserved, no poweroff
  Phase 2: Collect artifacts — logcat, dumpsys, pm list, APK hashes, fingerprint,
                               tombstones, SELinux, ADB config, process/netstate
  Phase 3: CVE match         — fingerprint → patch level → Android Security
                               Bulletin → kernel version → xairy index → component
                               hints from artifacts
  Phase 4: PoC correlation   — pocindex → GitHub → Exploit-DB → evaluate match
                               → reproduce in lab (0xbinder) if needed
  Phase 5: Attribution       — CVE → exploit chain structure → TAG/Project Zero
                               writeups → APK/sample comparison → limitations

DROPZER + GHOST:
  drozer  — app-layer IPC recon; maps what an attacker-controlled app can reach;
            does NOT exploit kernel bugs
  Ghost   — ADB post-exploitation; needs ADB access first; represents the
            post-exploit phase; useful for detecting post-compromise activity
            and understanding attacker foothold

================================================================================
END OF DOCUMENT
================================================================================

```

`android-kernel-exploit-dev-methodology.md`:

```md
# Android Kernel Exploit Development Methodology

## 1. From CVE to Exploit — Methodology

### 1.1 Bug Analysis: Classify the Primitive

The first step is to understand exactly what the bug lets you do. Classify the vulnerability into one of these primitive types:

**Use-After-Free (UAF):** A kernel object is freed but a pointer to it is retained. The attacker can reallocate the freed memory with attacker-controlled data, then operate on the dangling pointer to read/write kernel memory through the freed object's interface. The key question: what size is the freed object, what cache does it belong to, and what operations remain available through the dangling pointer?

**Out-of-Bounds (OOB) Read:** The kernel reads past the end of a buffer. This gives you an information leak primitive — you can read kernel memory that wasn't intended to be exposed. Adjacent slab objects, freelists, or kernel structures may contain pointers that leak KASLR offsets.

**Out-of-Bounds (OOB) Write:** The kernel writes past the end of a buffer. This gives you a controlled write primitive — you can corrupt adjacent object fields, function pointers, or slab metadata. The reliability depends on how predictable the adjacent memory is.

**Race / TOCTOU (Time-of-Check-Time-of-Use):** Two threads race on a check-then-use pattern. One thread passes a privileged check while another thread changes the condition before the use. Common in systems with permission checks that aren't atomic with the operation they guard.

**Info Leak:** A syscall, ioctl, or other interface returns kernel addresses or kernel memory contents to user-space without proper sanitization. This directly defeats KASLR or leaks sensitive data.

**Double-Free:** An object is freed twice. The second free may put the object on a freelist in a corrupted state, or the second free while the object is still in use creates a UAF condition. Often combined with UAF-style exploitation.

**Type Confusion:** The kernel treats an object as one type when it's actually another. This can give you arbitrary read/write if the confused type has different-sized fields or pointer accesses at different offsets.

### 1.2 Primitive Assessment: What Can You Build?

Given a raw bug, assess what useful operations you can construct:

- **UAF on a 128-byte object:** You regain allocation of a small object. You control its contents, and through the dangling pointer you can read/write 128 bytes of kernel memory at a time (or whatever the object's methods expose). Limited but useful if the object's methods let you read/write arbitrary kernel addresses through pointer fields in the object.

- **UAF on a 4KB object:** Much larger controlled region. More fields to manipulate, more methods available. But harder to groom — you need to fill a large hole, and other kernel activity may interfere.

- **OOB read on a small buffer:** You leak whatever is adjacent in memory. If the buffer is in a slab with other kernel objects, you leak their pointers. If it's in a special allocator region, you may leak metadata or adjacent user data.

- **OOB write on a small buffer:** You corrupt whatever is adjacent. If you can predict or control what's adjacent, you get a targeted write. If not, you get a random write that may crash the kernel.

- **Race on a privileged check:** You bypass the check. The primitive is "do operation X without having privilege Y." You still need to understand what operation X does and whether it gives you a more powerful primitive.

**The key question for each primitive:** what is the end state? Does it give you an info leak, a read primitive, a write primitive, code execution, or privilege escalation? Most real exploits chain multiple primitives: info leak → KASLR defeat → write primitive → cred overwrite → root.

### 1.3 Target Selection

Choose what you're exploiting against:

**Kernel version:** Pick a specific kernel version to target. The exploit may work across a range of versions if the bug exists in all of them and the kernel layout is stable enough. Check which Android devices ship with which kernel versions.

**Device/board:** Production devices vs. research devices. Production devices have the real mitigations and are the real target. Research devices (or QEMU-emulated kernels) are where you develop and test.

**Mitigations active:** Determine what's enabled on the target:
- Read `/proc/version` for kernel version
- Check `/proc/kallsyms` readability — if readable, KASLR defeat is trivial (some Android configs restrict this)
- Check `dmesg` for KASAN/KCFG/CFI messages
- Fingerprint mitigations via syscall behavior probes
- Understand whether PAN, PAC, STATIC_USERMODE_HELPER are active

**Production vs research:** Development happens on a research device (QEMU, or a test device with known config). The exploit is then validated against the target configuration. If the target has additional mitigations not present in your research environment, the exploit needs to account for them.

### 1.4 Exploit Primitive Construction

The classic pattern for turning a raw bug into a useful primitive:

**UAF → Arbitrary Read/Write:**
1. Trigger the UAF to free the target object
2. Reallocate the freed memory with attacker-controlled data (a "groomed" object that contains pointers or data you control)
3. Use the dangling pointer to read from or write to kernel memory through the object's interface
4. The dangling pointer gives you a kernel address; the reallocated content gives you control over what's read/written

**OOB Read → Info Leak:**
1. Trigger the OOB read to leak memory adjacent to the buffer
2. Parse the leaked data to extract kernel pointers
3. Use the pointers to calculate the kernel base address (KASLR defeat)
4. Combine with another primitive for the actual exploitation

**OOB Write → Controlled Write:**
1. Trigger the OOB write to corrupt adjacent memory
2. If you can predict what's adjacent, you get a targeted write to a useful target (function pointer, cred structure, etc.)
3. If you can't predict adjacency, you need a grooming step first to place a controlled object adjacent to the overflow buffer

**Race → Privilege Bypass:**
1. Set up two threads racing on the check-then-use
2. Thread A passes the check (e.g., permission validation)
3. Thread B changes the condition (e.g., frees and reallocates, changes a field) before Thread A uses the result
4. Thread A performs the privileged operation without the privilege

**Limited primitive → Full read/write:**
Real exploits rarely get arbitrary r/w directly. You get a limited primitive and chain it:
- Write 4 bytes at a controlled offset → overwrite a function pointer → get code execution → full r/w
- Read a kernel pointer → defeat KASLR → know where to write → overwrite target
- Corrupt a structure field → change behavior of a later operation → escalate

---

## 2. KASLR Defeat on Android

### 2.1 What KASLR Does

Kernel Address Space Layout Randomization randomizes the kernel base address at boot. Hardcoded kernel addresses in exploits don't work because the kernel is loaded at a different address each boot. The randomization applies to the kernel image itself — all kernel code and most kernel data are at offsets from the randomized base.

On Android ARM64, the kernel base is randomized with some entropy (the exact amount depends on architecture and kernel version). The randomization is per-boot, not per-process.

### 2.2 Android-Specific KASLR Behavior

- The kernel base is randomized at boot. All kernel code is at a fixed offset from this base.
- Some symbols and regions may be at fixed offsets from the base — the offset is constant, only the base moves.
- Module addresses: if kernel modules are loaded, their addresses may be randomized independently or may be at fixed locations depending on configuration. On production devices with module signing enforced, modules can't be loaded by exploits anyway.
- On some Android configurations, `/proc/kallsyms` is restricted (not readable by unprivileged users). On others, it may be partially or fully readable. Check the actual device state.

### 2.3 Info Leak Primitives That Defeat KASLR

You need a way to read a kernel pointer from user-space. Common sources:

**Unaddressed syscall return values:** Some syscalls return kernel pointers in their return value or in output buffers without sanitizing them. If a syscall returns a kernel address in a struct field that's passed back to user-space, that's an info leak.

**Slab object content:** Kernel objects in slabs contain pointers — freelists, back pointers, neighboring object pointers, function pointers. If you can read the content of a kernel object (through a UAF read, an OOB read, or a controlled read interface), you get kernel pointers.

**GPU/driver ioctl returns:** GPU drivers and other kernel drivers sometimes return kernel addresses in ioctl output buffers. These are common info leak sources because driver authors may not sanitize all output fields.

**Use-after-free reads:** If you have a UAF on an object that contains kernel pointers, and the object's read interface lets you read those pointers back to user-space, you get an info leak.

**`/proc/kallsyms` exposure:** On some Android configurations, `/proc/kallsyms` is readable (at least partially) by unprivileged users. If it's readable, you get all kernel symbols directly. Check actual device state — this varies by device, Android version, and vendor configuration.

**ksymtab patterns:** Even if `/proc/kallsyms` is restricted, some kernel symbols may be exposed through other paths. Some Android kernels expose a limited set of symbols needed for debugging or profiling.

### 2.4 How the Chain Works

1. **Info leak gives you a kernel address:** Through any of the above primitives, you leak a kernel pointer. The pointer is at a known offset from the kernel base (because kernel code/data are at fixed offsets from base, only the base is randomized).

2. **Calculate the kernel base:** Subtract the known offset from the leaked address to get the kernel base address. Now you know where the kernel is loaded in this boot.

3. **Find target symbols:** Add the known offset of your target symbol (e.g., `init_cred`, `prepare_creds`, `commit_creds`, syscall table entries, `system_state`) to the kernel base. Now you know the address of the thing you want to modify.

4. **Use write primitive to modify the target:** With the address known, your write primitive can target the correct location.

### 2.5 Static vs Randomized

Some things don't move with KASLR:
- If you can leak a pointer from a specific kernel object, and that object is at a known offset from a known reference point, you can calculate other things at known offsets from that reference.
- Module addresses may be at fixed locations in some configurations (though this is less common on modern Android).
- Some data structures are at fixed offsets from the kernel base — the offset is constant, the base moves.

The key insight: KASLR randomizes the base, not the internal layout. Once you know the base, you know everything.

---

## 3. Heap Feng Shui / Grooming on ARM64

### 3.1 Slab Allocator on Android

Android uses SLUB on most modern kernels (Android 10+ on ARM64 typically uses SLUB). SLUB has:

- **Per-CPU partial slabs:** Each CPU has a list of partially-filled slabs for each size cache. Allocations come from these partial slabs first.
- **Size-based caches:** Objects are allocated from caches based on their size. Objects of similar sizes may share a cache.
- **Order-0 allocations:** Most kernel objects are allocated with order-0 (one page) slabs. Larger objects may use higher orders.

Understanding the cache for the size of the object you're targeting is essential for grooming. You need to know what other objects share the same cache and how the allocator behaves.

### 3.2 Object Size Classes

SLUB caches objects by size. To groom effectively:

1. Determine the size of the target object (from kernel source, or by measuring through the bug).
2. Determine what size class that falls into (SLUB rounds up to the nearest size class).
3. Identify what other objects share that size class — they're your grooming candidates.
4. Allocate objects of the same size to fill the same cache.

If you can allocate the exact same object type as the target, that's ideal. If not, you allocate a different object type that falls into the same size cache — this is a cross-cache attack.

### 3.3 Grooming Sequence (Concrete Example)

Goal: place a controlled object adjacent to a target object in a slab, so that an OOB write on the controlled object corrupts the target, or a UAF on the target lets you read/write the controlled object.

**Step 1: Fill partial slabs.** Spray allocations of the target's size class to fill the per-CPU partial slabs. This ensures new allocations come from fresh slabs rather than reusing existing partial slabs.

```c
// Pseudocode: spray N allocations of the target size
for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) {
    void *obj = allocate_sized_object(TARGET_SIZE);
    save_for_later_free(obj);
}
```

**Step 2: Create holes.** Free specific allocations to create holes in the slabs. The holes should be positioned where you want your controlled object to land.

```c
// Free every other object to create alternating used/free pattern
for (int i = 0; i < N; i += 2) {
    free_sized_object(saved[i]);
}
```

**Step 3: Allocate controlled objects.** Allocate your controlled objects (the ones you'll use to trigger the bug) to fill the holes. Because of how SLUB allocates from partial slabs, your controlled objects land adjacent to the target objects.

```c
// Allocate controlled objects — they fill the holes
for (int i = 0; i < N/2; i++) {
    void *controlled = allocate_controlled_object();
    // This object is now adjacent to a target object in the slab
}
```

**Step 4: Trigger the bug.** Trigger the UAF, OOB, or other bug to operate on the target object. Because of the grooming, the target object is now adjacent to your controlled object, so the bug affects your controlled data.

### 3.4 Cross-Cache Attacks

When you can't allocate the exact same object type as the target:

1. Find an object type you CAN allocate that falls into the same size cache as the target.
2. Groom with that object type.
3. Trigger the bug on the target object type.
4. Because both object types share the same slab cache, your groomed objects are in the same slabs as the target objects.

This is common in Android kernel exploits because many different kernel objects share common size classes. Binder objects, file structures, socket buffers, and other kernel objects often share caches.

### 3.5 Heap Spraying vs Grooming

**Heap spraying:** Allocate many objects hoping one lands in the right place. Less reliable, more detectable (anomalous allocation patterns), but simpler to implement. PoCs often use spraying.

**Heap grooming:** Carefully arrange the heap so objects land where you want. More reliable, more work, but produces consistent results. Production exploits prefer grooming.

**Reliability considerations:**
- Per-CPU slab state: different CPUs may have different slab states. Your grooming may work on one CPU but not another.
- Concurrent kernel activity: other kernel threads may allocate/free concurrently, disrupting your grooming.
- Race conditions: if the bug involves a race, timing matters. Grooming must account for timing.
- Memory pressure: under memory pressure, the allocator behavior changes. Grooming that works in a idle system may fail under load.

---

## 4. Arbitrary Read/Write Primitive Construction

### 4.1 UAF → Arbitrary Read/Write

The classic UAF exploitation pattern:

1. **Free the target object.** Trigger the bug to free the object while a dangling pointer to it is retained by the kernel.
2. **Reallocate with attacker-controlled data.** Allocate a new object of the same size in the freed memory. This new object contains data you control — pointers, values, or structures designed to give you read/write capability.
3. **Use the dangling pointer.** The kernel still has a pointer to the freed (now reallocated) object. When it uses that pointer, it operates on your controlled data.
4. **Read/write kernel memory.** If the object's interface lets you read from or write to addresses specified in the object's fields, you can read/write arbitrary kernel memory. The dangling pointer gives you a kernel address; the reallocated content gives you control over what's read/written.

The specific capability depends on the object type and its methods. A UAF on a structure with a function pointer gives you code execution. A UAF on a structure with a pointer field that's dereferenced for reads gives you an arbitrary read. A UAF on a structure with a pointer field that's dereferenced for writes gives you an arbitrary write.

### 4.2 OOB Read → Info Leak

1. **Trigger the OOB read.** Read past the end of a buffer.
2. **Leak adjacent memory.** The memory past the buffer contains whatever is adjacent — other kernel objects, slab metadata, or whatever the allocator placed there.
3. **Extract pointers.** Parse the leaked data to find kernel pointers. These pointers are at known offsets from the kernel base.
4. **Defeat KASLR.** Calculate the kernel base from the leaked pointer.

If the OOB read is on a heap buffer, you leak heap contents. If it's on a stack buffer, you leak stack contents. The value of the leak depends on what's adjacent.

### 4.3 OOB Write → Controlled Write

1. **Trigger the OOB write.** Write past the end of a buffer.
2. **Corrupt adjacent memory.** The write lands on whatever is adjacent to the buffer.
3. **Target specific objects.** With grooming, you can place a specific object adjacent to the buffer so the OOB write corrupts a targeted field in that object.
4. **Achieve a useful write.** If you corrupt a function pointer, you get code execution. If you corrupt a cred pointer, you get privilege escalation. If you corrupt a length field, you may get a more powerful read/write primitive.

Without grooming, the OOB write is random — you corrupt whatever happens to be adjacent. This may crash the kernel or may accidentally give you a useful primitive. Grooming makes it deterministic.

### 4.4 Race/TOCTOU → Privilege Bypass

1. **Identify the check-then-use pattern.** Find where the kernel checks a condition (permission, size, state) and then uses the result of that check.
2. **Set up the race.** Two threads: one passes the check, the other changes the condition before the use.
3. **Win the race.** Thread A passes the check. Thread B changes the condition (frees and reallocates, modifies a field, changes a state variable). Thread A uses the stale check result.
4. **Achieve the bypass.** The operation proceeds without the privilege or condition that was supposed to guard it.

Race exploitation is timing-sensitive. On a slow system or under load, the race window may be too small. On a fast system or with careful timing, the race is winnable. Production exploits for race bugs often include multiple attempts and adaptive timing.

### 4.5 Limited Primitive → Full Read/Write

You rarely get arbitrary r/w directly. Common paths from limited to full:

**Write 4 bytes at a controlled offset → overwrite a function pointer → code execution → full r/w:**
- You have a primitive that writes a controlled value at a controlled offset in a structure.
- You target a function pointer in that structure.
- When the function pointer is called, you control the instruction pointer.
- From code execution, you can do anything — read/write arbitrary memory, escalate credentials, etc.

**Read a kernel pointer → defeat KASLR → know where to write → overwrite target:**
- You have a primitive that reads kernel memory at a controlled offset.
- You read a kernel pointer to defeat KASLR.
- Now you know the address of your target (init_cred, function pointer, etc.).
- You use a write primitive (even a limited one) to target the known address.

**Corrupt a structure field → change behavior of a later operation → escalate:**
- You corrupt a field that controls behavior (e.g., a callback pointer, a size field, a flag).
- A later operation uses the corrupted field in a way that gives you a more powerful primitive.
- Chain from the limited primitive to the powerful one.

### 4.6 Common Targets for the Write Primitive

When you have a write primitive (even a limited one), these are the common targets:

**`current->cred` or `current->real_cred`:** Overwrite to point to `init_cred`. The process's credentials are reread from these pointers on each permission check. If they point to init_cred (root), the process is root.

**`init_cred`:** The credential structure for PID 1 (init), which runs as root (UID 0, GID 0, full capabilities). If you can get your process to use init_cred, you're root. Directly modifying init_cred affects all processes that reference it.

**`prepare_creds` / `commit_creds`:** The safe way to change credentials. `prepare_creds` creates a new cred structure based on the current one; `commit_creds` applies it. Exploits sometimes call these directly if they have code execution. Overwriting these function pointers gives you control over credential changes.

**Syscall table entries:** Overwrite a syscall handler to get code execution when that syscall is invoked. The syscall table is an array of function pointers — overwriting one gives you controlled execution.

**Function pointers in kernel structures:** Many kernel structures contain function pointers for callbacks, handlers, or methods. Overwriting any of these gives you controlled code execution when the pointer is called.

**`system_state` or similar global state variables:** Some global variables control kernel behavior. Modifying them can disable protections or change behavior in your favor.

**Task struct fields:** The task struct contains UID, GID, credential pointers, and other fields related to process identity. Modifying these directly can escalate privileges, though it's less reliable than full cred overwrite because the kernel may revalidate from the cred structure.

---

## 5. Android Mitigation Landscape

### 5.1 KASAN (Kernel Address Sanitizer)

**What it does:** Detects UAF, OOB, double-free, use-after-return at runtime. When KASAN detects a bug, it crashes the kernel immediately with a detailed report showing exactly where the bug occurred.

**Exploitation impact:** If KASAN is enabled, many bugs crash the kernel on detection rather than giving an exploitable primitive. The bug is detected before you can exploit it.

**Production status:** Production devices typically do NOT run with KASAN enabled. KASAN has significant performance overhead (memory usage, runtime checks) that's unacceptable for production. Research devices and lab builds may run with KASAN for bug discovery and debugging.

**Exploit development implication:** Develop against a KASAN-enabled kernel to find and understand the bug. Then test against a non-KASAN kernel that matches the production configuration. The exploit must work without KASAN catching it.

### 5.2 KCFG (Kernel Control Flow Guard)

**What it does:** Restricts indirect calls to valid targets. Before an indirect call, KCFG checks that the target is a valid function address. This makes it harder to hijack function pointers and call arbitrary kernel code.

**Exploitation impact:** If KCFG is enabled, overwriting a function pointer with an arbitrary address may fail the KCFG check at call time. The call is blocked, and the kernel may crash or fall back to a safe path.

**Production status:** Enabled on some Android kernels, more common on Android 12+. Not universally enabled — depends on kernel config and vendor choices.

**Exploitation implication:** If KCFG is active, you can't just overwrite any function pointer with an arbitrary address. You need to target a function pointer whose target passes the KCFG check, or find a path that doesn't go through KCFG-checked calls.

### 5.3 CFI (Control Flow Integrity, Compiler-Level)

**What it does:** Clang CFI enforces type-based constraints on indirect calls. Similar goal to KCFG but implemented at the compiler level. Indirect calls are checked against the expected type signature.

**Exploitation impact:** Makes ROP and jump-to-arbitrary-address harder. If you overwrite a function pointer, the CFI check may fail if the target doesn't match the expected type.

**Production status:** Enabled on some Android kernels, particularly those built with Clang. More common on newer Android versions.

### 5.4 PAN (Privileged Access Never)

**What it does:** ARM64 feature that prevents the kernel from accessing user-space pointers unless explicitly allowed. Without PAN, a kernel exploit that gets an arbitrary write could write to user-space memory directly — the kernel can access user pointers transparently. With PAN, user pointers are blocked unless the kernel explicitly marks them as accessible.

**Exploitation impact:** PAN affects how you get data into and out of user-space during exploitation. Without PAN, you can write directly to user-space memory from the kernel. With PAN, you need techniques like pt_regs manipulation or other methods to get data across the kernel/user boundary.

**Production status:** Generally enabled on modern Android ARM64 kernels. Requires ARMv8.1+ or specific implementation support.

**Exploitation implication:** If PAN is active, your exploit can't simply write to a user-space buffer from kernel code. You need to use kernel interfaces that explicitly allow user-space access, or manipulate pt_regs to temporarily disable PAN for a specific access.

### 5.5 STATIC_USERMODE_HELPER

**What it does:** Restricts kernel code from jumping directly to user-space instructions. Some older exploitation techniques relied on the kernel returning to or jumping to user-controlled code. STATIC_USERMODE_HELPER prevents this.

**Exploitation impact:** Affects techniques that relied on returning to user-space code from the kernel. Modern exploits typically don't rely on this — they use kernel function pointers or other kernel-side execution — but it's a mitigation to be aware of.

### 5.6 KPTI/PTI (Kernel Page Table Isolation)

**What it does:** Separates kernel and user page tables. The kernel has its own page table that includes kernel mappings; user processes have page tables that don't include kernel mappings (or include them only when the kernel is running).

**Exploitation impact:** Primarily relevant on x86 (Meltdown mitigation). ARM64 behavior differs — ARM64 typically uses a different approach to isolate kernel and user memory. On ARM64, KPTI is less of a factor for exploitation because the architecture handles kernel/user separation differently.

### 5.7 Module Signing

**What it does:** Prevents loading unsigned kernel modules. Traditional kernel exploitation (load a rootkit module) is blocked. Exploits must work within the existing kernel, not load new code.

**Exploitation impact:** This is the norm on production Android devices. You can't load a kernel module to get code execution. You must exploit the existing kernel code and data.

**Exploitation implication:** All exploitation must be done through existing kernel interfaces, bugs, and primitives. No loading custom code into the kernel.

### 5.8 Why Production Devices Have a Subset

Production devices don't run all mitigations because:

- **Performance overhead:** KASAN is too expensive for production. Other mitigations have varying overhead.
- **Hardware support:** PAN requires ARMv8.1+ or specific implementation. PAC requires ARMv8.3+. Older devices may not support all mitigations.
- **Vendor-specific config choices:** Different vendors enable different mitigations based on their risk assessment, hardware, and Android version.
- **Different Android versions have different defaults:** Android 10, 11, 12, 13, 14 each have different mitigation defaults.

An exploit for a production device needs to work with whatever mitigations that device actually has. This means:
- Testing against the specific device or a close approximation.
- Having fallback paths for different mitigation configurations.
- Understanding which mitigations are likely present on the target.

---

## 6. ARM64 Specifics

### 6.1 Pointer Authentication (PAC)

**What it is:** ARMv8.3+ feature that signs pointers with a key that's inaccessible to user-space. The kernel can sign pointers (using instructions like `paciasp`, `pacia`, etc.) and verify the signature before using them. If the signature doesn't match, the pointer is rejected.

**Kernel PAC usage:** On kernel code, PAC can protect return addresses and function pointers. Android kernel PAC usage depends on version and config. Some kernels sign return addresses (so ROP is harder), some sign function pointers (so overwriting function pointers is harder), some do both.

**Exploitation impact:** If PAC is enabled on the function pointers you want to overwrite, a naive overwrite may fail signature verification when the pointer is used. You need to either:
- Target function pointers that aren't PAC-protected.
- Find a way to sign your pointer (requires control of the PAC key, which is kernel-only).
- Use a different exploitation path that doesn't rely on overwritten function pointers.

**Key question:** Does PAC apply to your target? Check the kernel config and the specific function pointers you're targeting. Not all function pointers are PAC-protected — it depends on how the kernel code was compiled and which pointers were signed.

### 6.2 ARM64 Calling Conventions

- **Arguments:** First 8 arguments in x0-x7.
- **Return value:** x0.
- **Caller-saved registers:** x0-x15 (including x0-x7 for arguments, x8 for indirect result, x9-x15 for temporary values).
- **Callee-saved registers:** x19-x28 (preserved across function calls).
- **Frame pointer:** x29 (FP).
- **Link register:** x30 (LR) — stores the return address for function calls.

**For kernel exploitation:**
- If you can control a function call's arguments (via overwriting a function pointer and controlling what calls it, or via a primitive that lets you set up a call), you control x0-x7.
- If you can get code execution (via overwriting a function pointer, ROP, or other means), you control the full register state, including x0-x30.
- The link register (x30) is critical for ROP — if you can overwrite x30 or control what value is in x30 when a function returns, you control the instruction pointer.

### 6.3 ARM64 vs ARM32

**ARM32:**
- Uses ARM and Thumb instruction sets (interchangeable at switching points).
- Calling convention: r0-r3 for first 4 arguments, r0 for return value. r4-r11 are callee-saved, r12 (IP) is caller-saved, r13 (SP) is stack pointer, r14 (LR) is link register, r15 (PC) is program counter.
- Different register names and sizes (32-bit vs 64-bit).
- Some mitigations differ (PAC is ARM64-specific, PAN behavior differs).

**ARM64 is more common on modern devices.** Most Android devices from the last several years are ARM64. ARM32 kernel exploitation is largely the same concepts but with different register names, different calling conventions, and some different mitigation availability.

---

## 7. Cred Manipulation — The Classic Root Escalation

### 7.1 init_cred

`init_cred` is the credential structure for PID 1 (init), which runs as root: UID 0, GID 0, full capabilities, SELinux context appropriate for init.

If you can get your process to use `init_cred`, you're root. The process's credentials are checked on each permission operation — if the credentials say root, the operation is allowed.

### 7.2 prepare_creds / commit_creds

The safe, intended way to change credentials:

- **`prepare_creds(struct cred *old`):** Creates a new cred structure based on the current one. Returns a new cred that can be modified.
- **`commit_creds(struct cred *new`):** Applies the new cred to the current process. The process's credentials are replaced with the new ones.

Exploits sometimes call these directly if they have code execution — call `prepare_creds`, modify the returned cred to be root, call `commit_creds`. This is the cleanest escalation path.

Some exploits bypass these and directly overwrite `current->cred` to point to `init_cred`. This is faster but less clean — the kernel doesn't go through the proper credential transition, and some code may behave differently. In practice, directly overwriting `current->cred` works on most kernels.

### 7.3 Overwriting current->cred

If you have an arbitrary write primitive:

1. Leak or calculate the address of `init_cred` (using KASLR defeat).
2. Overwrite `current->cred` to point to `init_cred`.
3. Optionally also overwrite `current->real_cred` to point to `init_cred`.
4. The process's credentials are now root. Permission checks read from these pointers and see root credentials.

The process's credentials are reread from these pointers on each permission check. Once you overwrite them, all subsequent permission checks see root.

### 7.4 UID/GID Manipulation

Directly overwriting `current->uid`, `current->gid`, `current->suid`, `current->sgid`, `current->euid`, `current->egid` to 0.

Less reliable than full cred overwrite because the kernel may revalidate from the cred structure rather than reading the individual fields. On some kernels, the individual uid/gid fields are just cached values and the real authority is the cred structure. On others, overwriting the fields directly works.

In practice, full cred overwrite (`current->cred` → `init_cred`) is preferred because it's more reliable across kernel versions.

### 7.5 SELinux Considerations

Even with root UID, SELinux may confine you:

- **Enforcing mode:** Being root (UID 0) doesn't give you unrestricted access — you're still subject to SELinux policy. A root process in the wrong SELinux context may be denied operations that an unconfined root could do.
- **Permissive mode:** SELinux logs denials but doesn't enforce them. Root is effectively root.
- **Exploit implications:** On enforcing devices, being root may not be sufficient. Exploits may need to also disable SELinux or work within the SELinux context. Some exploits target SELinux disabling as a separate step (e.g., writing to `/sys/fs/selinux/enforce` if accessible, or modifying SELinux kernel state).

Check the device's SELinux mode. On permissive devices, root escalation is sufficient. On enforcing devices, additional steps may be needed.

---

## 8. Case Studies of Recently Written Android Kernel Exploits (2023-2026)

### 8.1 CVE-2019-2215 (Binder UAF)

The classic teaching example of Android kernel exploitation.

**Bug:** UAF in binder transaction handling. Binder is Android's IPC mechanism — it's a kernel driver that handles inter-process communication. The bug is in how binder transactions are processed: a transaction object is freed while a pointer to it is retained, creating a UAF condition.

**Exploitation approach:**
1. Groom the heap with binder objects of the right size.
2. Trigger the UAF to free a binder transaction object.
3. Reallocate the freed memory with attacker-controlled data (a groomed binder object).
4. Use the dangling pointer to read/write through the freed object.
5. Escalate credentials using the obtained primitive.

**Why it's the "hello world":** Binder is available on all Android devices, the bug is well-understood, and the exploitation path is documented. The 0xbinder lab has a full walkthrough with QEMU setup, GDB debugging, and step-by-step exploitation. It teaches the fundamental patterns: heap grooming, UAF exploitation, KASLR defeat, cred manipulation.

**Key learning:** Binder is a rich target because it's a complex kernel driver that handles user-space inputs, maintains kernel state, and has many internal structures. Bugs in binder are often exploitable because binder objects are allocatable from user-space and the driver exposes multiple interfaces.

### 8.2 CVE-2023-20938 (Binder ioctl UAF)

A more recent binder vulnerability. Google Android OffSec blog has exploitation details.

**Bug:** UAF in binder ioctl handling. Similar to CVE-2019-2215 but in a different code path within the binder driver. The bug is triggered through specific ioctl calls that create a UAF condition.

**Exploitation approach:** Similar to CVE-2019-2215 — groom with binder objects, trigger the UAF, reallocate with controlled data, use the dangling pointer. The specifics of the grooming and trigger differ based on the exact bug.

**Why binder continues to be a target:** Binder's complexity and privileges make it a recurring source of kernel vulnerabilities. It handles complex IPC state, runs in kernel context, and processes inputs from user-space. Each new binder code path or feature introduces potential bugs.

### 8.3 CVE-2023-20938 + CVE-2023-2136 Chain

Project Zero and Android OffSec analyzed in-the-wild exploits using these CVEs together.

**The chain:** CVE-2023-20938 provides one primitive (e.g., info leak or write primitive), and CVE-2023-2136 provides another. Together they give a more complete exploitation capability than either alone.

**Pattern:** Info leak from one CVE defeats KASLR. Write primitive from another CVE overwrites the target (cred, function pointer, etc.). The two CVEs complement each other — one gives you the address, the other gives you the write.

**Lesson:** Real exploits often chain multiple CVEs. A single bug may give you a limited primitive; chaining with another bug gives you full exploitation capability.

### 8.4 Other Binder CVEs

- **CVE-2023-21255:** Another binder vulnerability. Binder has been a recurring source of kernel vulnerabilities because of its complexity and privileged execution context.
- **CVE-2023-20938:** Already discussed above.

Binder's track record as a vulnerability source is strong. Any binder bug should be considered potentially exploitable.

### 8.5 GPU/Driver CVEs

**CVE-2023-26083 (Mali tlstream info leak):** Information leak in Mali GPU driver's tlstream handling. GPU drivers are a rich target because they run in kernel context, handle user-space inputs (graphics commands, shader data), and have complex state machines. Info leaks from GPU drivers can defeat KASLR.

**CVE-2025-21479 (Adreno GPU driver):** Vulnerability in Adreno GPU driver. Adreno is Qualcomm's GPU; the kernel driver handles GPU commands from user-space. GPU driver bugs can give various primitives depending on the exact bug.

**Why GPU drivers are targets:**
- They run in kernel context with high privileges.
- They handle complex user-space inputs (graphics commands, memory buffers, shader programs).
- They have large, complex codebases with many state machines.
- They're present on all Android devices (every device has a GPU).
- They expose ioctl interfaces that user-space can call.

### 8.6 CVE-2022-20409 (io_uring)

 io_uring is a Linux async I/O interface. The CVE is a kernel privilege escalation via io_uring.

**Relevance to Android:** io_uring is a general Linux kernel feature, not Android-specific. It's available on Android kernels that include it. This shows that non-Android-specific kernel features can be exploited on Android too — you don't need an Android-specific bug.

**Exploitation approach:** io_uring bugs typically involve the complex state machine of io_uring operations, ring buffer handling, or callback processing. The exploitation follows the general kernel exploitation pattern: understand the bug, construct a primitive, escalate.

### 8.7 CVE-2022-0847 (Dirty Pipe)

Pipe buffer flag manipulation for arbitrary write. Not Android-specific but relevant to kernel exploitation on Android.

**Bug:** A bug in pipe buffer handling that allows writing to pages that should be read-only. By manipulating pipe buffer flags, an attacker can write to arbitrary kernel memory (specifically, pages mapped in the kernel).

**Exploitation approach:** Use the pipe primitive to write to kernel memory. The write is limited (page-level granularity, specific conditions) but can be used to overwrite critical kernel data.

**Relevance:** Shows that even bugs in seemingly simple kernel subsystems (pipes) can be exploitable. Pipe bugs are accessible from user-space and can give powerful primitives.

### 8.8 Info Leak CVEs

**CVE-2023-0266 (ALSA compat layer race, in-the-wild Samsung):** An info leak in the ALSA (sound) compatibility layer. A race condition in the compat layer leaks kernel addresses. This was used in-the-wild on Samsung devices.

**CVE-2023-26083 (Mali info leak):** Already discussed above. GPU driver info leak.

**Why info leaks matter:** They defeat KASLR, which is the first step in most kernel exploits. Without KASLR defeat, you don't know where to write. Info leaks are often the first primitive in a chain.

---

## 9. Tooling

### 9.1 QEMU + Vulnerable Kernel

The standard research environment for Android kernel exploit development.

**Setup:** Run a vulnerable kernel in QEMU. The kernel is configured to expose the bug you're exploiting. QEMU provides a reproducible, debuggable environment.

**Why QEMU:**
- Reproducible: same kernel, same config, same behavior every run.
- Debuggable: GDB can attach to the QEMU-emulated kernel.
- Isolated: bugs that crash the kernel don't affect your host system.
- Controlled: you can enable/disable mitigations as needed for testing.

**Reference:** See `android-kernel-exploit-lab-setup.md` (CVE-2019-2215 lab) for a concrete QEMU setup walkthrough.

### 9.2 GDB + Kernel Debugging

Attach to QEMU-emulated kernel, set breakpoints, inspect memory and registers, step through exploit code.

**Essential for:**
- Understanding the bug: step through the vulnerable code path, see what happens.
- Developing the exploit: set breakpoints at key points, inspect heap layout, verify grooming.
- Debugging failures: when the exploit doesn't work, GDB shows you why.

**Common GDB workflows:**
- `break` at the vulnerable function to see the bug in action.
- `print` kernel structures to understand their layout.
- `x` (examine memory) to inspect heap layout and verify grooming.
- `info registers` to see register state at key points.
- `step`/`next` to walk through the exploitation sequence.

### 9.3 KASAN-Enabled Kernel Builds

Build the kernel with KASAN to detect the bug when developing/testing.

**Workflow:**
1. Build the kernel with KASAN enabled.
2. Trigger the bug — KASAN crashes the kernel with a detailed report showing exactly where the bug occurred (stack trace, object sizes, allocation/free history).
3. Use the KASAN report to understand the bug precisely.
4. Develop the exploit against a non-KASAN kernel (which is what production devices run).

**Why this matters:** KASAN tells you exactly what the bug is, what objects are involved, and what the allocation pattern is. This information is essential for constructing the exploit. But the exploit must work against a non-KASAN kernel — KASAN would catch the bug and crash before exploitation.

### 9.4 Kernel Config Analysis

Understand what's enabled in the target kernel:

**Key config options:**
- `CONFIG_SLUB` — SLUB allocator (most common on Android).
- `CONFIG_KASAN` — KASAN enabled (research/debug, not production).
- `CONFIG_KCFG` — KCFG enabled (control flow guard).
- `CONFIG_CFI` — Clang CFI enabled.
- `CONFIG_ARM64_PAN` — PAN enabled.
- `CONFIG_STATIC_USERMODE_HELPER` — restricts user-space jumps.
- `CONFIG_MODULES` — module loading enabled.
- `CONFIG_MODULES_SIG` — module signing enforced.
- `CONFIG_KASLR` — KASLR enabled.
- `CONFIG_ARM64_PAC` — PAC enabled (if applicable).

**How to check:**
- Read `/proc/config.gz` if available (some Android kernels expose the config).
- Check kernel build artifacts if you have access to them.
- Fingerprint via syscall behavior: try operations that would behave differently under different configs.
- Check dmesg for messages about enabled features.

### 9.5 syzkaller

Kernel fuzzer that finds bugs. Generates syscalls, discovers UAF/OOB/race conditions.

**What it does:** syzkaller generates random and semi-random syscall sequences, runs them, and detects crashes, hangs, and other anomalies. It's particularly good at finding race conditions and memory corruption bugs.

**Relevance to exploit development:**
- Understanding syzkaller output helps understand bug patterns.
- syzkaller-generated reproducers can be starting points for exploit development.
- Google uses syzkaller to find kernel bugs, including Android kernel bugs.

**Limitations:** syzkaller finds bugs but doesn't write exploits. The exploit development is a separate step.

### 9.6 kallsyms / ksymtab

Kernel symbol tables.

**`/proc/kallsyms`:** If readable, gives you all kernel symbols and their addresses. This directly defeats KASLR — you get the kernel base and all symbol addresses.

**On restricted Android configs:** `/proc/kallsyms` may be hidden (not readable by unprivileged users). But some symbols may still leak through other paths:
- Some Android kernels expose a limited set of symbols needed for debugging or profiling.
- Info leaks from bugs may give you kernel pointers.
- ksymtab patterns: even without `/proc/kallsyms`, the kernel's internal ksymtab may be accessible through other means.

**Check actual device state:** Don't assume `/proc/kallsyms` is restricted. Check if it's readable. If it is, KASLR defeat is trivial. If it's not, you need an info leak primitive.

---

## 10. Writing Robust Exploits

### 10.1 Reliability

A PoC that works 1/10 times demonstrates the bug. A production exploit works 9/10+ times. Reliability comes from:

**Careful heap grooming, not spraying:** Spraying is unreliable — you hope an allocation lands in the right place. Grooming arranges the heap so allocations land where you want. Grooming is more work but more reliable.

**Accounting for per-CPU state:** Different CPUs have different slab states. Your grooming may work on one CPU but not another. Account for this by grooming on the specific CPU you'll trigger the bug on, or by making the grooming work regardless of CPU.

**Handling races:** If the bug involves a race, the timing must be right. Use adaptive timing — try different timing parameters, retry on failure, adjust based on system load.
