```md
## 10. Open-Source Tooling Workflow for Attribution

A practical workflow a researcher can execute with free/accessible tools. This is research-scale, not enterprise SOC scale.

### Step 1: Get the Sample

- Obtain the APK from:
  - VirusTotal (if available in their corpus).
  - MalwareBazaar (download if available).
  - AndroZoo (search and download).
  - Your own collection.
- Record the source, the date you obtained it, and any metadata (first-seen date, tags, detections).

### Step 2: Extract Static Features

- Decompile with JADX or apktool.
- Extract:
  - Package name.
  - Signing certificate hash (SHA-256).
  - Strings: C2 domains, IPs, URLs, email addresses, command names, configuration keys.
  - API usage patterns: which Android APIs are called, especially sensitive ones (SMS, contacts, overlay, accessibility, camera, microphone).
  - Manifest structure: components, permissions, intent filters.
  - Native library info: which .so files are included, their exports, their hashes (for later comparison).
  - Resource patterns: distinctive images, layouts, strings that might be reused across samples.

### Step 3: Extract Dynamic Features (if you can run it safely)

- Run in a sandboxed device or emulator with network capture.
- Extract:
  - C2 infrastructure: domains contacted, IPs contacted, URLs requested, protocols used.
  - Behavior profile: what data is collected, what APIs are used, what is sent where.
  - Beaconing patterns: timing, frequency, payload format.
- If you can't run it safely, rely on VirusTotal's behavioral report (if available) or MalwareBazaar's behavioral data.

### Step 4: Search VirusTotal

- Search by APK hash.
- Look at:
  - Detection results (which engines flag it, what names they assign).
  - Relationship graph (related files — same certificate, similar files).
  - Behavioral report (if available — C2 infrastructure, behavior profile).
  - Detection timeline (when first submitted, how detections evolved).

### Step 5: Search MalwareBazaar

- Search by hash.
- If found: check tags, family assignments, first-seen date, related samples.
- If not found: your sample may be new to their corpus, or may not be in their collection.

### Step 6: Search AndroZoo

- Search by package name, certificate hash, or detection pattern.
- Find related samples — especially ones with the same certificate or package name pattern.
- This expands your sample set for clustering.

### Step 7: Certificate Transparency Search

- For each domain found in the sample (static or dynamic):
  - Search crt.sh for certificates issued for that domain.
  - Search for the certificate fingerprint to find other domains sharing the same certificate.
  - Note the issuance dates and organization names.

### Step 8: WHOIS/DNS History

- For each campaign domain:
  - Look up current WHOIS (whois command or web service).
  - If you have access to historical WHOIS or passive DNS, look up historical data.
  - Note registration dates, name servers, registrar, any registrant info (if not privacy-protected).
  - Check passive DNS for historical IP associations and co-hosted domains.

### Step 9: Write YARA Rules

- Based on extracted features, write YARA rules targeting this family:
  - String-based rules: C2 domains, distinctive strings, certificate hashes (as hex strings).
  - Package name patterns.
  - API call patterns (if you can express them in YARA — limited but possible for .so files).
- Scan your expanded sample set (from AndroZoo, VirusTotal, MalwareBazaar) with these rules.
- Find additional family members.

### Step 10: Cluster and Classify

- Cluster the found samples using:
  - Certificate hash groupings.
  - Package name similarity.
  - C2 infrastructure overlap.
  - String overlap.
- Identify family boundaries — which samples are clearly the same family, which are related, which are outliers.
- Compare against known families from vendor reports. Does your cluster match a known family? Does it appear to be a new variant or a distinct family?

### Step 11: Document the Campaign

- Document what you know:
  - Family name (your internal name, cross-referenced with vendor names if applicable).
  - Timeframe: first-seen to last-seen in your collection, plus infrastructure dates.
  - Infrastructure: domains, IPs, certificates, hosting.
  - Capabilities: what the malware does.
  - Targeting: which apps, which regions, which populations.
  - Monetization mechanism: SMS fraud, banking, ad fraud, crypto, spyware — whatever the capabilities indicate.
  - Attribution assessment: family-level (low confidence), campaign-level (medium confidence), or actor attribution (high confidence — and if so, what additional evidence supports it beyond samples).
  - What you can't determine: be explicit about the gaps.

---

## 11. Honest Limitations

These limitations are not edge cases — they are the normal state of Android malware attribution.

### Attribution is Hard

- Even with good evidence, attribution can be wrong.
- Operators can use false flags (fake language clues, fake code comments, fake infrastructure patterns designed to mislead).
- Operators can share infrastructure with other operators (intentionally or incidentally).
- Operators can buy, sell, or rent infrastructure, making infrastructure-based attribution ambiguous.
- Operators can copy, buy, or license code from other groups.
- Attribution is often revised when new evidence emerges. Treat your own attribution claims with the same skepticism you apply to vendor claims.

### Sample Access is Biased

- You only see samples that were collected, analyzed, and made available.
- A campaign may operate on victims whose samples never enter your collection — because the victims weren't identified, because the samples weren't submitted to sandboxes, because the malware was used in a targeted way that avoided broad detection.
- Your timeline is a lower bound. Your campaign scope is a partial view.
- Different collections have different biases — VirusTotal skews toward samples that were submitted (often by security vendors or automated systems), AndroZoo skews toward apps that were crawled (more coverage of alternative markets, less of targeted attacks), MalwareBazaar skews toward recent samples.

### Infrastructure is Ambiguous

- Same IP can host many unrelated domains — cloud IPs, shared hosting, CDNs.
- Same hosting provider is extremely common — millions of domains share the same large providers.
- Same WHOIS registrant can be a privacy proxy service used by millions of domain owners.
- One infrastructure signal alone is almost always weak. Multiple corroborating signals are stronger but still not definitive.

### Code Reuse is Ambiguous

- Code can be copied without the original author's involvement (leaked code, pasted snippets, stolen code).
- Code can be sold or licensed between groups.
- Code can be shared through common toolkits or protectors without the operators being the same.
- Significant code overlap is suggestive but not definitive. The weight it carries depends on how distinctive the code is — generic API usage patterns are weak evidence; distinctive, non-generic code is stronger.

### Family Naming is Inconsistent

- Different vendors name the same family differently. This is a known, persistent problem.
- A family called "Mamont" by Kaspersky may be called something else by Lookout or Check Point.
- Cross-referencing requires comparing capabilities, infrastructure, code, and targeting — not just family names.
- If you work with multiple vendor feeds, build your own mapping based on your analysis.

### Attribution Claims Require Evidence Beyond Samples

- Claims about specific actors — criminal groups, state actors, individuals — require evidence that sample analysis alone cannot provide.
- That evidence may include: law enforcement investigations, intelligence community sources, legal process outcomes (domain registrar records with unredacted registrant info, financial records), victim reports across multiple organizations, infrastructure seizures.
- If you don't have access to that evidence, your attribution claims should be appropriately scoped: family identification, campaign description, infrastructure mapping — not actor identification.

### State Actor Attribution from Mobile Malware Samples Alone is Insufficient

- When mobile malware is attributed to state actors in public reports, the evidence typically comes from a broader investigation that includes:
  - Non-mobile indicators (network traffic, phishing infrastructure, other malware).
  - Intelligence sources (human intelligence, signals intelligence, partnerships with other governments).
  - Victimology across sectors (not just mobile victims — email, network, cloud).
  - Sometimes infrastructure seizures or law enforcement actions.
- Mobile malware sample analysis alone, no matter how sophisticated, is rarely sufficient for state actor attribution. The sample may be consistent with state actor activity, but the positive attribution requires the broader evidence base.
- Be particularly careful with state actor attribution claims. The stakes are higher, the evidence bar should be higher, and the opportunity for misattribution (including false flags by sophisticated actors) is real.

---

## 12. Known Recent Android Malware Campaigns (2025-2026)

This section documents specific campaigns and families identified in 2025-2026 research. These are family-level identifications — not actor attribution. For methodology on how to link samples to campaigns, see Section 2.

### 12.1 ClayRat — Self-Propagating Spyware (Oct-Dec 2025)

- **First identified:** October 2025 by Zimperium
- **Targeting:** Russian users
- **Scale:** Over 700 unique APKs detected in 3 months, over 600 samples in initial 3-month window
- **Distribution:** 25+ phishing domains, Dropbox hosting, Telegram channels, SMS self-propagation to all contacts after infection
- **Capabilities (original):** Default SMS handler abuse to bypass permission prompts, steal SMS/call logs/notifications, front-camera photos, mass SMS to all contacts (self-propagation via contact list), remote commands (take photos, list apps, make calls)
- **Capabilities (upgraded variant v3.0.8, Dec 2025):** Added Accessibility Services — keylogger (PIN/password/pattern capture + auto-unlock), screen recording via MediaProjection API, fake overlays (system update, battery, PIN capture overlay), fake notification replies intercept, programmatic button tapping to prevent power-down/uninstall
- **Technical notes:** AES/CBC encrypted payload in assets folder. Alternate variant uses AES-GCM encrypted C2 traffic with dynamic loading of encrypted payload from assets. Obfuscates payloads by inserting "apezdolskynet" marker into Base64-encoded data. Communicates over HTTP.
- **Why it matters:** Demonstrates dual-privilege abuse (SMS default handler + Accessibility), self-propagation via contact list, and anti-tampering (blocks uninstall/power-off). Scale (700+ builds in months) shows active development.
- **Sources:** Zimperium blog (return-of-clayrat-expanded-features-and-techniques), SecurityAffairs (clayrat-campaign-uses-telegram-and-phishing-sites-to-distribute-android-spyware)
- **Confidence:** HIGH — Zimperium + SecurityAffairs primary sources

### 12.2 Arsink RAT — Cloud-Native Mass Spyware (2025-2026)

- **First identified:** 2025 by Zimperium zLabs
- **Scale:** 1,216 distinct APK hashes, 774 with Google Apps Script uploader, 317 Firebase RTDB endpoints, 45,000 unique victim IPs across 143 countries. 4 operational variants identified.
- **Targeting:** Largest concentrations in Egypt (~13K), Indonesia (~7K), Iraq (~3K), Yemen (~3K), Turkey (~2K)
- **Distribution:** Telegram, Discord, MediaFire links, impersonating 50+ brands (Google, YouTube, WhatsApp, Instagram, Facebook, TikTok). "Mod"/"Pro" versions of legitimate apps.
- **Capabilities:** Device snapshot + identifiers (public IP, model, OS, battery, coarse location, Google account emails), SMS harvesting + continuous exfiltration, call logs, contacts, microphone recordings, photos + arbitrary files, remote control (flashlight, vibration, audio, wallpaper, toasts, TTS, CALL_PHONE, file ops, destructive wipe)
- **C2 infrastructure:** Google Apps Script + Firebase Realtime Database + Firebase Storage + Google Drive + Telegram (one variant). 317 unique Firebase RTDB endpoints as C2/data sinks. Google Apps Script for large file exfiltration to Google Drive. Firebase Storage for audio.
- **Why it matters:** Cloud-native C2 — abuses legitimate Google infrastructure (Firebase, Apps Script, Drive) rather than dedicated C2 servers. This makes takedown harder and infrastructure harder to distinguish from legitimate use. Scale (45K IPs, 143 countries) indicates mass-distribution campaign.
- **Takedown:** Zimperium worked with Google to dismantle malicious Firebase endpoints + Apps Script instances. Google disabled malicious Apps Script accounts. Confirmed Arsink not on Google Play; Play Protect protects users. Attackers forced to retool — demonstrates multi-infrastructure redundancy (4 variants with different exfiltration paths).
- **Sources:** Zimperium blog (the-rise-of-arsink-rat)
- **Confidence:** HIGH — Zimperium zLabs primary source

### 12.3 PromptSpy — AI-Powered Android Malware (Feb 2026)

- **First identified:** February 10, 2026 by ESET Research (variant). Earlier variant VNCSpy identified January 13, 2026 (Hong Kong).
- **What it is:** First known Android malware to use generative AI (Google Gemini) in its execution flow — for persistence, not core malware function.
- **How it works:** Sends Gemini a natural-language prompt + XML dump of current screen. Gemini returns JSON instructions for what gesture to perform (tap coordinate, etc.) to keep app locked in recent apps list. Feedback loop: malware confirms with Gemini until AI validates app is locked. This replaces hardcoded screen coordinates/taps/UI selectors that break across devices, OS versions, and manufacturer skins.
- **Distribution:** Fake Chase Bank (JPMorgan Argentina) site → dropper (MorganArg) → PromptSpy payload
- **Other capabilities:** VNC module (screen viewing + remote actions), lockscreen data capture, uninstall blocking, device info gathering, screenshots, screen video recording
- **Status:** Not yet observed in ESET telemetry — possibly proof of concept. But distribution domain exists, suggesting real targeting (Argentina).
- **Why it matters:** New evasion paradigm — AI-guided behavior replaces hardcoded UI selectors. Device-agnostic persistence. Signals a direction where malware uses GenAI APIs at runtime for dynamic, adaptive behavior. Not yet in widespread telemetry but conceptually significant.
- **Sources:** ESET Research (welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/promptspy-ushers-in-era-android-threats-using-genai)
- **Confidence:** HIGH — ESET primary research. Note: proof-of-concept status means persistence/propagation unknown.

### 12.4 Albiriox — MaaS Android Banking RAT (Dec 2025)

- **First observed:** September 2025, recruitment phase started
- **Model:** Malware-as-a-Service (MaaS) — full-featured Android Remote Access Trojan + banking Trojan sold to criminals
- **Targeting:** Initially Austria, then expanding globally. Targets 400+ banking/crypto/fintech apps.
- **Capabilities:** Live screen streaming to attacker (real-time tap/swipe/type), on-device fraud (transactions via victim's own banking session), Accessibility Services abuse, overlay attacks (under development), black-screen masking to hide attacker activity from victim
- **Why it matters:** MaaS model lowers barrier for entry-level criminals. Live remote control + on-device fraud bypasses MFA and device fingerprinting. Structured architecture with loaders, modules, control panels. Represents commodification of sophisticated banking fraud capabilities.
- **Sources:** Malwarebytes blog (new-android-malware-lets-criminals-control-your-phone-and-drain-your-bank-account)
- **Confidence:** HIGH — Malwarebytes primary source, IOCs provided

### 12.5 Zimperium 4 Campaigns — RecruitRat, SaferRat, Astrinox (Mirax), Massiv (2025-2026)

- **Source:** Zimperium Banking Heist Report / zLabs research
- **Scale:** 800+ targeted apps across banking, crypto, and social media
- **Common TTPs across all 4 campaigns:**
  - Multi-stage installation with Session Installation API abuse to bypass sideloading/accessibility restrictions
  - Droppers disguised as Google Play Store updates
  - Non-interactive overlays during permission grants
  - Structural APK tampering
  - Overlay attacks, fake lock screens
  - Package discovery without QUERY_ALL_PACKAGES permission
  - Near-zero detection rates against traditional signature-based security
  - Play Store update droppers for persistence
- **Individual campaigns:**
  - **RecruitRat:** Fake recruitment/job-seeking platforms as distribution
  - **SaferRat:** Fake premium streaming access websites
  - **Astrinox (aka Mirax by Zimperium, aka Medusa by Cleafy):** Mimics HireX via xhire[.]cc domain. Serves APK for Android, App Store impersonation for iOS (though no iOS IoCs found). Also tracked as "Medusa" by Cleafy Labs.
  - **Massiv:** Distribution infrastructure could not be definitively identified — decoupled from core malware logic
- **Why it matters:** Demonstrates sophisticated multi-stage installation techniques that bypass recent Android security restrictions. Near-zero signature detection means traditional AV approaches are insufficient. Play Store update droppers represent a new persistence vector.
- **Sources:** Zimperium (android-bankers-4-campaigns-in-a-row), Cleafy Labs (Medusa press release)
- **Confidence:** HIGH for Zimperium findings; MEDIUM for Medusa/Cleay details (page returned near-empty content)

---

## 13. Bottom Line

Attribution and campaign tracking on Android malware is a research discipline that combines sample analysis, infrastructure investigation, and threat intelligence correlation. It is valuable for detection, blocking, threat hunting, and understanding the threat landscape — even when you can't identify the human operator behind it.

You can determine, from samples and accessible tools:

- Family relationships (code similarity, cert reuse, C2 overlap, string/resource reuse).
- Campaign timeframes (sample dates, versioning, C2 lifecycle).
- Infrastructure footprints (domains, IPs, hosting, certificates).
- Monetization mechanisms (SMS fraud, banking, ad fraud, crypto, spyware).

You cannot reliably determine, from sample analysis alone:

- The human operator.
- Their location or affiliation.
- The full campaign scope.
- The financial beneficiaries.

The tools you can actually use are VirusTotal, MalwareBazaar, AndroZoo, YARA + clustering scripts, Certificate Transparency (crt.sh), WHOIS/DNS history services, and simple code similarity approaches (SimHash, ssdeep, manual Smali comparison). The case study references are vendor research portals — Kaspersky Securelist, Zimperium Banking Heist Report, Lookout, Check Point Research, Trend Micro, ESET, Palo Alto Unit 42, CrowdStrike, Microsoft Security, and Google TAG — and academic literature on family classification and clustering.

Use these tools to build defensible, useful conclusions about what a sample is, what campaign it belongs to, what infrastructure it uses, and what it's designed to do. Be honest about what the evidence supports and what it doesn't. And when you see attribution claims — from vendors, from yourself, from anywhere — ask what evidence supports them and whether that evidence is sufficient for the claim being made.

That honesty is not a limitation of the methodology. It is the methodology.
```

```md
# Android Malware C2 Infrastructure Analysis — Research Collection

*Curated sources and notes on Android malware C2 infrastructure analysis tooling and methodology.*

## Reality check

Direct, dedicated open-source tooling for Android malware C2 infrastructure analysis is thin in the way the user suspects: most repos in this space are research writeups, manual-analysis workflows, or repurposed network-analysis tooling. Reusable, turnkey OSS that maps C2 infrastructure for Android families end-to-end is uncommon. The practical value is in combining: APK static/network extraction, network traffic analysis, indicator correlation, and infrastructure-reuse research.

## Sources

- **eml2http** — https://github.com/sbousseaden/eml2http — EML/MHTML to HTTP reconstruction tool by Steve Bousseaden. Primary use is email-based sample triage and extraction of embedded HTTP/URL artifacts; useful as a precursor step when C2 configuration is embedded in samples, payloads, or email-delivered droppers.
- **reversinglabs/RLater** — https://github.com/reversinglabs/RLater — ReversingLabs malware analysis engine. Modular pipeline approach to static/dynamic analysis; can be adapted for C2 indicator extraction from Android samples as part of a broader triage flow.
- **RedNote mobile security articles** — recent Android/iOS device security coverage; not Android-C2-specific but relevant for indicator and infrastructure context from mobile threat research.
- **Cybercrime Tracker** — https://github.com/elastic-cybercrime/cybercrime-tracker — Elastic's cybercrime infrastructure tracking; infrastructure-level indicators, campaign mapping, and related context useful when cross-referencing Android C2 infrastructure with broader threat activity.
- **tcostam/awesome-command-control** — https://github.com/tcostam/awesome-command-control — index of C2 tools and infrastructure concepts; includes open-source C2 server examples and infrastructure ideas; used here as conceptual background, not production Android C2 tooling.

## Notes

- ReversingLabs research on malware abusing public infrastructure (GitHub Gists, git commit messages, public services) is directly relevant to understanding modern C2 patterns, even when the original writeup is not Android-specific.
- Social-media-as-C2 and server-less C2 research is relevant because Android malware increasingly blends into "normal-looking" infrastructure and protocols; manual trace-and-correlate work is still central.
- For Android-specific C2 infrastructure analysis, the strongest practical path is usually: (1) extract URLs/URIs/strings/configs from APK or payload; (2) reconstruct or capture traffic; (3) correlate domains, IPs, certificates, and account patterns; (4) cross-reference infrastructure-reuse across samples and campaigns.

## Gaps

- No single turnkey OSS Android-C2-infrastructure-triaging pipeline surfaced in this collection; most capable approaches remain workshop-style or vendor-backed.
- Dedicated Android-C2-simulation/emulation tooling is limited and often overlaps with general Android test C2 or red-team tooling, which may not match analysis/triage intent.
```

`android-malware-c2-infra-analysis.md`:

```md
# Android Malware C2 Infrastructure Analysis — Deliverable

*Synthesized from deleg_a3b40756 + existing SKILL.md corpus + web research. Written 2026-09-04.*

## 1. Reality check on dedicated OSS tooling

There is no single turnkey open-source pipeline that does end-to-end Android malware C2 infrastructure analysis out of the box. The practical space is split across several smaller things: sample triage and extraction tooling, network traffic reconstruction, indicator correlation, and infrastructure-reuse research. Most capable workflows are workshop-style or vendor-backed rather than one OSS repo you install and run.

What does exist and is useful:

- **eml2http** — https://github.com/sbousseaden/eml2http — EML/MHTML-to-HTTP reconstruction; useful when C2 configuration or payload fetch paths are embedded in email-delivered droppers or sample artifacts. Primary author: Steve Bousseaden.
- **ReversingLabs RLater** — https://github.com/reversinglabs/RLater — malware analysis pipeline that can be adapted for static/dynamic indicator extraction, including C2 indicators, as part of a broader triage flow.
- **Cybercrime Tracker** — https://github.com/elastic-cybercrime/cybercrime-tracker — Elastic's cybercrime infrastructure tracking; useful for cross-referencing Android C2 infrastructure with broader threat activity and campaign mapping.
- **awesome-command-control** — https://github.com/tcostam/awesome-command-control — index of C2 tooling and infrastructure concepts; useful as conceptual background and for understanding how C2 architectures are typically built, not as Android-specific analysis tooling.

## 2. C2 infrastructure patterns relevant to Android malware

Modern Android malware C2 is often deliberately dull and blended:

- Use of HTTPS to normal-looking domains.
- Use of public infrastructure such as GitHub, Gists, cloud storage, and version-control features as command or payload channels.
- Short-lived or frequently rotated domains, IPs, and accounts.
- Infrequent or low-volume beaconing to blend into normal traffic.
- Multi-stage retrieval: initial fetch points to secondary infrastructure, which fetches further payloads or configuration.

This matters because infrastructure analysis for Android malware is often less about finding a custom C2 protocol and more about tracing ordinary-looking communication back to its operator-controlled meaning.

## 3. Practical workflow for C2 infrastructure analysis

### 3.1 Extract candidate indicators from the sample

Start from the APK or payload and pull out candidate C2 artifacts:

- URLs, URIs, and partial domains embedded in code or resources.
- Hardcoded IPs, ports, and connection strings.
- Config-loading patterns, remote-configuration fetchers, and update checks.
- Encoded or obfuscated strings that resolve to network endpoints at runtime.
- Certificate pins, user-agent strings, and other communication fingerprints.

Tooling angle:

- Static extraction via Androguard, JADX, apktool, MobSF.
- String and resource inspection for encoded or layered configuration.
- APKLeaks-style thinking for finding hidden URLs and sensitive artifacts in resources/assets.

### 3.2 Reconstruct or capture traffic

If possible, observe the malware talking:

- Run the sample in an instrumented or emulated environment and capture traffic.
- Use frida-based MITM or unpinning where HTTPS gets in the way of seeing the actual destination and payload.
- Extract and reconstruct HTTP/S flows from captured artifacts when available.

Tooling angle:

- frida-interception-and-unpinning-style scripts for HTTPS visibility.
- Emulated/sandboxed execution with traffic capture.
- Eml2http-type reconstruction where communication artifacts are embedded in sample-based artifacts.

### 3.3 Correlate infrastructure

Once you have indicators, correlate:

- Domains, IPs, certificates, account names, and naming patterns across samples.
- Shared infrastructure reuse across campaigns or families.
- Changes over time: new fallback domains, moved payload hosts, rotated accounts.

Tooling angle:

- Threat-intel correlation: MalwareBazaar, VirusTotal, passive DNS/WHOIS context where available.
- Infrastructure tracking like Elastic's cybercrime tracker for broader context.
- Manual correlation is often still central because Android-specific C2 infrastructure is frequently blended with generic attacker infrastructure.

### 3.4 Map to campaign and operator patterns

The point of C2 infrastructure analysis is usually not just "what domain did it call home" but:

- Whether the infrastructure is shared with other activity.
- Whether the communication pattern fits a known campaign style.
- Whether the payload fetch, beacon, or config update behavior matches a broader operator pattern.

This is where writeups such as the ReversingLabs research on malware abusing public infrastructure matter: they show how modern C2 can live inside normal services and workflows, which changes how you trace it.

## 4. Sandbox and dynamic analysis tooling notes

### What's realistically useful

Sandbox-style Android analysis is best thought of as "instrumented execution + behavior capture," not a single product you run.

Useful building blocks:

- Instrumented or emulated execution environments for observing runtime behavior.
- Dynamic instrumentation to reveal hidden network activity, especially when pinning, encryption, or runtime-only configuration is involved.
- Static pre-analysis to know which behaviors and endpoints are worth watching dynamically.
- Network capture and HTTP flow reconstruction to turn runtime behavior into analyzable artifacts.

### Gaps

- There is no dominant open-source Android malware sandbox that also does polished C2 infrastructure mapping as a first-class feature.
- A lot of the "good" sandboxing workflow is still assembled from pieces: unpack/decode, run, intercept, capture, correlate.
- For serious infrastructure-level analysis, vendor tools and manual research still carry much of the load.

## 5. Lightweight frameworks worth knowing

These are not full C2 infrastructure platforms, but they are genuinely useful and lightweight pieces for Android security work that can feed C2-related analysis:

- **MobSF** — static and some dynamic analysis; good first-pass extraction of manifest, permissions, API usage, URLs, and other indicators that often point toward C2 behavior.
- **Androguard** — programmable APK/DEX inspection for custom extraction of C2-relevant strings, endpoints, and behavior patterns.
- **frida-interception-and-unpinning** and related Frida scripts — network visibility when apps hide traffic behind pinning or native TLS.
- **APKiD + APKLeaks-style checks** — fast identification of packers/protectors and hidden artifacts that may contain C2 configuration.

Lightweight does not mean complete. It means easy to stand up and useful as part of a larger workflow.

## 6. Bottom line

For Android malware C2 infrastructure analysis, treat the problem as a process rather than a tool:

1. Extract indicators from the sample.
2. Make the traffic visible where possible.
3. Correlate infrastructure across samples and time.
4. Map patterns to campaigns and operator behavior.

The strongest practical posture is a combination of static triage, dynamic interception, traffic reconstruction, and manual infrastructure correlation, supported by threat-intel sources and infrastructure-tracking projects rather than a single OSS C2-analysis product.

```

`android-malware-detection-research.md`:

```md
# Android Malware Detection & Stealthy-Behavior Hunting — Research Summary
# Generated: 2026-09-04

================================================================================
SECTION 1 — MOST USEFUL TOOL REPOS
================================================================================

1. MobSF (Mobile Security Framework)
   github.com/mobsf/mobile-security-framework-mobsf
   All-in-one static + dynamic Android/iOS analysis. Auto-extracts manifest,
   permissions, API calls, hardcoded secrets, certificate pinning, and runs
   YARA against the APK. REST API + CLI for CI/CD. The single most complete
   open-source Android malware analysis platform. Matters because it replaces
   a whole toolchain with one deployable stack.

2. Androguard
   github.com/androguard/androguard
   Python toolkit for APK/DEX/odex reverse engineering. Parses manifests,
   extracts permissions, services, receivers, providers, intent filters,
   decompiles DEX to Python AST, and has a built-in YARA module. Used as the
   analysis engine inside MobSF and many other tools. Matters because it's
   the programmatic backbone for custom APK triage scripts.

3. APKLeaks
   github.com/dwisiswant0/apkleaks
   Scans APK for hardcoded URLs, endpoints, API keys, secrets, and tokens.
   Fast, CLI-driven, regex-based. Matters for quick triage of suspicious
   apps before deeper analysis — often surfaces C2 URLs and exfil endpoints.

4. MARA Framework
   github.com/xtiankisutsa/MARA_Framework
   Mobile Application Reverse engineering and Analysis framework. Combines
   static + dynamic analysis with configurable rules. Less mature than MobSF
   but useful as a secondary opinion and for custom plugin development.

5. Androwarn
   github.com/maaaaz/androwarn
   Static code analyzer focused on malicious-behavior detection in Android
   apps. Scores behaviors like data exfiltration, telephony abuse, SMS fraud,
   stealth techniques. Outputs HTML/JSON reports. Matters for automated
   behavior scoring when you don't want full MobSF.

6. MVT (Mobile Verification Toolkit)
   github.com/mvt-project/mvt
   Consensual forensic acquisition + IOC scanning for Android/iOS devices.
   Pulls backup/ADB artifacts and checks them against IOC lists (package
   names, signatures, domains, file hashes). Maintained by Amnesty
   International Security Lab. Matters for on-device compromise detection,
   not just APK analysis.

7. Pithus
   beta.pithus.org
   Open-source APK analyzer with YARA-based malware hunting. Static analysis
   only (beta). Matters as a lightweight web-based alternative when you want
   a quick shared analysis without running a local instance.

8. Androl4b
   github.com/sh4hin/Androl4b
   VM appliance for Android reverse engineering and malware analysis. Comes
   pre-configured with apktool, jadx, Frida, MobSF, and other tools.
   Matters when you want a throwaway analysis environment without setup.

9. Android Security Awesome (curated list)
   github.com/ashishb/android-security-awesome
   Comprehensive curated list of Android security tools, online analyzers,
   static/dynamic analysis tools, malware datasets, and research papers.
   Matters as the index to everything else.

================================================================================
SECTION 2 — ON-DEVICE TRIAGE WORKFLOW FOR STEALTHY MALWARE
================================================================================

GOAL: Find stealthy malware hiding on a live Android device — hidden
receivers/services, dynamic code loading, clickers, adware, spyware.

PREREQUISITES:
- USB debugging enabled (or root for deeper inspection)
- ADB installed on analysis machine
- Optional: Frida server running on device for runtime inspection

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 0 — Quick "is this device compromised?" screen
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. Check for known stalkerware/spyware packages:
   $ adb shell pm list packages -3                # all third-party apps
   $ adb shell pm list packages | grep -iE "spy|track|monitor|record|stealth|parent|secure|control|guard"

   Cross-reference against IOC lists:
   - github.com/AssoEchap/stalkerware-indicators (package names, domains)
   - github.com/mvt-project/mvt-indicators (spyware campaign IOCs)

2. Check accessibility services (malware abuse vector):
   $ adb shell settings get secure enabled_accessibility_services
   Look for unknown packages. Clicker/adware/spyware often abuses this.

3. Check for overlay/window permission abuse:
   $ adb shell dumpsys activity | grep -A5 "mShowDialerSwitch"
   $ adb shell dumpsys window | grep -i "permission"

4. Check battery usage anomalies:
   $ adb shell dumpsys batterystats --charged
   Look for unknown packages consuming background CPU/wake locks.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 1 — Enumerate ALL components (including "disabled" ones)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Malware often declares components but sets enabled="false" in manifest
to evade static scanners, then enables them at runtime.

1. Full component dump:
   $ adb shell dumpsys package <package.name>

   Look for:
   - <service> entries with enabled=false but exported=true
   - <receiver> entries for hidden intents (SMS_RECEIVED, BOOT_COMPLETED,
     PACKAGE_INSTALLED, TIME_TICK, NETWORK_STATUS)
   - <provider> entries with unfamiliar content URIs
   - <activity> entries with android:exported="true" and no icon

2. Check for components that are "disabled" but have
   android:process=":remote" or unique process names — signs of
   hidden background services.

3. Check for multiple processes from one package:
   $ adb shell ps -A | grep <package.name>
   Malware often spawns a hidden worker process.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 2 — Detect dynamic code loading (DCL)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

DCL lets an app load .dex/.apk/.jar at runtime, evading static analysis.

1. Check APK for DCL patterns:
   - Decompile with apktool: apktool d app.apk
   - Search smali for:
     * dalvik/system/DexClassLoader
     * dalvik/system/PathClassLoader
     * java/lang.ClassLoader.loadClass()
     * java/net/URLClassLoader
   $ grep -r "DexClassLoader\|PathClassLoader\|loadClass" smali/

2. Check for APK expansion / OBB / asset files that contain dex:
   $ unzip -l app.apk | grep -E "\.dex|\.apk|\.jar"

3. Monitor runtime DCL with Frida (if device is rooted/jailbroken):
   - Hook java.lang.ClassLoader.loadClass()
   - Hook dalvik.system.DexClassLoader
   - Log every class loaded at runtime, especially from external storage

4. Check for downloaded payload files:
   $ adb shell ls -la /data/local/tmp/
   $ adb shell ls -la /sdcard/Download/
   $ adb shell ls -la /sdcard/Android/data/<package>/
   Malware often drops dex/apk payloads into external storage and loads them.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 3 — Detect clickers, adware, and SMS/telephony fraud
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. Check for tap/jitter/click automation:
   - Look forAccessibilityService usage in manifest (malware uses this to
     simulate taps)
   - Check for classes using android.view.InputEvent, MotionEvent,
     Instrumentation.sendPointerSync(), or Robot pattern in smali
   $ grep -r "sendPointerSync\|MotionEvent\|Instrumentation" smali/

2. Check for adware / aggressive notification abuse:
   $ adb shell dumpsys notification
   Look for excessive notifications from unknown packages.
   $ adb shell pm list permissions -g -d | grep -i "notification\|post_notifications"

3. Check SMS/telephony fraud patterns:
   - Hidden SMS_RECEIVED receiver that intercepts OTPs
   - Hidden CALL_PHONE / SEND_SMS permissions
   - Services that make background calls
   $ adb shell dumpsys package <pkg> | grep -i "permission"
   Look for: READ_SMS, SEND_SMS, RECEIVE_SMS, CALL_PHONE, READ_CALL_LOG,
   READ_CONTACTS, ACCESS_FINE_LOCATION

4. Check for premium SMS / silent SMS sending:
   - Frida hook android.telephony.SmsManager.sendTextMessage()
   - Or check smali for SmsManager usage

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 4 — Detect spyware / data exfiltration
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. Check permissions and actual usage:
   $ adb shell dumpsys package <pkg> | grep -A20 "granted=true"
   Look for dangerous permission combos: READ_CONTACTS + READ_SMS +
   ACCESS_FINE_LOCATION + RECORD_AUDIO + CAMERA all together.

2. Check for background camera/microphone usage:
   - Check for CameraManager, MediaRecorder, AudioRecord usage in smali
   - Check for invisible camera preview (1x1 pixel view)
   $ grep -r "CameraManager\|MediaRecorder\|AudioRecord\|SurfaceView" smali/

3. Check for contact/calendar/sms exfiltration:
   - ContentResolver queries on contacts, call logs, SMS URIs
   - Network operations (HttpURLConnection, OkHttp, retrofit) sending
     data to external domains
   $ grep -r "ContentResolver\|query.*contacts\|query.*sms\|query.*call_log" smali/
   $ grep -r "http\|https\|socket\|OkHttp\|Retrofit" smali/

4. Frida runtime inspection:
   - Hook java.net.HttpURLConnection, java.net.Socket to log outgoing
     connections from suspicious packages
   - Hook camera/microphone APIs
   - Hook SMS/telephony APIs
   - Dump inter-process communication (Binder) if malware uses multiple
     processes

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 5 — Check for persistence and stealth techniques
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. Boot persistence:
   $ adb shell dumpsys package <pkg> | grep -i "boot\|android.intent.action.BOOT_COMPLETED"
   Check for RECEIVE_BOOT_COMPLETED permission + BOOT_COMPLETED receiver.

2. Package installation persistence:
   Check for PACKAGE_INSTALLED / PACKAGE_ADDED receivers — malware can
   reinstall itself or check for competing security apps.

3. Icon hiding:
   - Check for android.intent.action.MAIN with
     android:label="@android:string/empty" or no icon
   - Check for ComponentInfo flags hiding from launcher:
     $ adb shell dumpsys package <pkg> | grep -i "launcher\|icon\|component"

4. Check for Device Administrator abuse:
   $ adb shell dumpsys device_policy
   Malware can use DeviceAdminReceiver to prevent uninstallation.

5. Check for hidden APKs (installing without user knowledge):
   $ adb shell pm list packages -3
   Compare against what's visible in launcher. Packages with
   android:exported="false" and no launcher activity won't show.

6. Check for overlay attacks (tapjacking, phishing overlays):
   $ adb shell dumpsys activity | grep "mToken"
   Look for TYPE_LAYER_TYPE_HARDWARE or floating overlay windows from
   suspicious apps.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 6 — Network and forensic correlation
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

1. Check device logs for suspicious activity:
   $ adb logcat -d | grep -iE "error|fail|exception|malware|spy|unknown"

2. Pull and analyze APK for deeper static analysis (if obtainable):
   $ adb shell pm path <package.name>
   $ adb pull /data/app/~~/base.apk
   Then run through MobSF, androguard, jadx, YARA.

3. Check for suspicious installed but hidden apps using:
   $ adb shell pm list packages -3 | sort > installed_third_party.txt
   $ adb shell pm list packages -s | sort > installed_system.txt
   Cross-check system packages against known-good lists.

4. Use MVT for IOC-based device scan:
   $ mvt-android check-backup --iocs iocs.json /path/to/backup
   Or use MVT's adb-based acquisition for live device:
   $ mvt-android check-device --iocs iocs.json

================================================================================
SECTION 3 — YARA RULE SOURCES AND ANDROID USAGE
================================================================================

RULE REPOSITORIES:

1. VirusTotal / YARA (upstream engine + sample rules)
   github.com/VirusTotal/yara
   The canonical YARA engine. Includes sample.rules. VirusTotal also
   runs YARA against their entire corpus via Livehunt and Retrohunt.
   Custom vt module exposes file metadata in rules.

2. YARA-X (VirusTotal's Rust rewrite)
   github.com/VirusTotal/yara-x
   ~99% rule-compatible with classic YARA, memory-safe, faster.
   Currently used in production for Livehunt/Retrohunt at VT.
   Version 1.19.0 as of 2026.

3. InQuest / Awesome-YARA
   github.com/InQuest/awesome-yara
   Curated list of YARA rules, tools, and researchers. Links to many
   malware-family-specific rule sets. Good starting point for discovery.

4. bartblaze/Yara-rules
   github.com/bartblaze/Yara-rules
   Broad collection of rules for various malware families. Includes
   package action to download all rules as one file.

5. ReversingLabs Open-Source YARA Rules
   reversinglabs.com/open-source-yara-rules
   Rules covering downloaders, trojans, ransomware, exploits, viruses.
   Maintained by ReversingLabs.

6. MalwareBazaar YARA statistics
   bazaar.abuse.ch/export/
   Shows which YARA rules match which malware samples. Useful for
   understanding which rules are actively matching in the wild.

7. Elastic protections-artifacts
   github.com/elastic/protections-artifacts
   YARA rules from Elastic's detection engineering. High-quality,
   well-maintained, mapped to MITRE ATT&CK.

8. Krypteria/Yaralyze (mentioned in context)
   github.com/Krypteria/Yaralyze
   Client-server YARA + hash static analysis. 130k rules / 500k hashes.
   Tested against Brata/Sharkbot/Cerberus/Flubot. Dated 2022 — rules
   may be stale.

9. anpa1200/Android-Malware-Analysis (mentioned in context)
   APK -> androguard -> YARA -> semantic scoring -> ATT&CK -> Frida ->
   JSON report. Good integrated pipeline example.

10. Koodous Androguard YARA module
    docs.koodous.com/yara/androguard.html
    Uses Androguard to extract Android-specific features and match
    against YARA rules. Integrates APK analysis with YARA matching.

ANDROID-SPECIFIC YARA PATTERNS TO LOOK FOR:

- Package name patterns of known malware families
- Hardcoded C2 URLs, IP addresses, domain strings in res/values/strings.xml
- API key / token strings
- suspicious permissions strings in AndroidManifest.xml
- Known malware certificate fingerprints / signature hashes
- Cryptominer battery-drain patterns
- Bank trojan overlay layout patterns
- SMS/telephony fraud strings ("SEND_SMS", premium number patterns)
- Clickers: motion event simulation strings
- Spyware: camera/recorder permission abuse strings
- DexClassLoader / dynamic loading strings in smali

HOW TO USE YARA ON ANDROID:

A) Scan APK files (static):
   $ yara -r rules/ /path/to/apk/dir/
   Run against extracted APK contents (smali, dex, assets, manifest).

B) Scan extracted APK contents:
   $ apktool d app.apk -o extracted/
   $ yara -r rules/ extracted/

C) Scan Android backup / forensic images:
   $ yara -r rules/ /path/to/backup/
   MVT can incorporate YARA scanning during forensic acquisition.

D) Scan ADB-pulled APKs from device:
   $ for pkg in $(adb shell pm list packages -3 | cut -d: -f2); do
       adb shell pm path $pkg | cut -d: -f2 | xargs -I{} adb pull {} /tmp/apks/
     done
   $ yara -r rules/ /tmp/apks/

E) VirusTotal Livehunt / Retrohunt:
   Upload YARA rules to VT to scan their corpus; useful for discovering
   new samples matching your rules.

F) YARA rules for Android should target:
   - AndroidManifest.xml (binary XML — use apktool to decode first,
     or write rules against the binary XML patterns)
   - smali/dex code patterns
   - res/values/strings.xml for URLs, keys, suspicious texts
   - assets/ and lib/ for native library patterns
   - META-INF/ for signing certificate anomalies

LIMITATIONS:
- YARA on raw APK is limited because AndroidManifest.xml is binary XML.
  Decode first with apktool or write rules for binary XML byte patterns.
- YARA doesn't understand Dalvik bytecode semantics — it's byte/string
  matching only. Combine with androguard/MobSF for semantic analysis.
- Many Android malware families share little static overlap; behavioral
  analysis (Frida, dynamic) catches what YARA misses.

================================================================================
SECTION 4 — STALKERWARE / SPYWARE IOC DATASETS AND DETECTION SCRIPTS
================================================================================

IOC DATASETS:

1. Stalkerware Indicators of Compromise
   github.com/AssoEchap/stalkerware-indicators
   Comprehensive list of stalkerware/spyware apps with package names,
   domains, URLs, and hosting infrastructure. ~80+ stalkerware families
   listed including: mSpy, FlexiSpy, Cocospy, Spyic, PanSpy, TheOneSpy,
   TheTruthSpy, uMobix, androidmonitor, Fenced, LetMeSpy, EasyPhoneTrack,
   Spyzie, and many others. Includes C2 domains and affiliate domains.
   This is the most directly useful IOC list for on-device triage.

2. MVT Indicators (mvt-project/mvt-indicators)
   github.com/mvt-project/mvt-indicators
   IOC lists for known spyware campaigns (Pegasus, Mercenary, etc.)
   used by MVT for device scanning. Focused on nation-state / mercenary
   spyware, not commercial stalkerware, but valuable for high-end threats.

3. Android Malware Genome Project
   malgenomeproject.org
   1,200+ malware samples from 2010-2011 covering majority of families
   at that time. Historical but foundational.

4. Drebin Dataset
   user.informatik.uni-goettingen.de/~darp/drebin/
   5,560 apps from 179 malware families (2010-2012). Classic academic
   dataset. Labels reliable. Still used for ML model training.

5. AndroZoo
   androzoo.uni.lu
   5.7M+ APKs from Google Play and other sources, each scanned by AV
   engines. Regularly updated. Largest publicly available Android app
   corpus. Includes VT detection counts per sample — useful for labeling.

6. VirusShare
   virusshare.com
   Malware sample repository. Has Android samples. Access requires
   registration. Samples can be used to build YARA rules.

7. MalwareBazaar (abuse.ch)
   bazaar.abuse.ch
   Malware samples with YARA rule statistics. Growing Android coverage.
   API access for sample download.

8. Kharon Malware Dataset
   kharon.gforge.inria.fr/dataset/
   7 deeply reverse-engineered Android malware samples with documentation.
   Small but well-documented.

9. AAGM Dataset (CIC)
   Unb.ca CIC Android Adware dataset
   1,900 apps captured on real smartphones semi-automated. Adware-focused.

DETECTION SCRIPTS / TOOLS:

1. ISDi (Initial Smart Device Inspector)
   Developed by Havron et al. (2019), Clinic to End Tech Abuse (CETA),
   Cornell Tech. Scans Android/iOS for stalkerware based on package names
   and known indicators. Designed for survivors of intimate partner
   violence. Tool is referenced in academic literature; check CETA's
   GitHub for current availability.

2. WARNE (Stalkerware Evidence Collection Tool)
   ScienceDirect article (2023). Evidence collection and analysis tool
   for stalkerware. Companion to ISDi work.

3. MVT (Mobile Verification Toolkit)
   github.com/mvt-project/mvt
   Forensic acquisition + IOC scanning. Can detect known spyware
   packages, suspicious accessibility services, device admin abuse,
   and other compromise indicators on live or backed-up devices.

4. STOP STALKERWARE — SpyGuard
   stopstalkerware.org
   Resources and guidance for detecting digital abuse. Not a technical
   tool but provides detection methodology.

5. Android Stalkerware (diskurse)
   github.com/diskurse/android-stalkerware
   Additional stalkerware repository with analysis.

PRACTICAL STALKERWARE DETECTION ON DEVICE:

Step 1: List all third-party packages:
$ adb shell pm list packages -3 > all_third_party.txt

Step 2: Cross-reference against stalkerware-indicators list.
   Many stalkerware apps use package names like:
   - com.mspy.app
   - com.fleksy.android.spy
   - com.theonespy.android
   - com.spynote.android
   - com.ultimatephonespy.*
   - com.androidmonitor.*
   - com.cocospy.*
   - com.amosspy.*
   etc. (full list in AssoEchap/stalkerware-indicators)

Step 3: Check for apps with no launcher icon but running services:
$ adb shell pm list packages -3
$ adb shell dumpsys activity | grep "NoDisplay\|exported.*false\|component"

Step 4: Check for Device Administrator apps:
$ adb shell dumpsys device_policy
   Stalkerware often registers as device admin to prevent removal.

Step 5: Check for Accessibility Service abuse:
$ adb shell settings get secure enabled_accessibility_services
   Many stalkerware apps require accessibility to read notifications,
   SMS, and screen content.

Step 6: Check for Device Owner / Profile Owner (MDM abuse):
$ adb shell dumpsys device_policy | grep -i "owner\|profile"

Step 7: Check for hidden/system apps installed from user space:
   Some stalkerware disguises itself as a system app or hides its icon.
   Compare pm list packages against visible app drawer contents.

Step 8: Use MVT for IOC-based scanning if you have a backup:
   $ mvt-android check-backup --iocs combined_iocs.json backup.tar

Step 9: For deeper analysis, pull suspicious APKs and analyze:
   $ adb shell pm path <suspect.package>
   $ adb pull <apk_path>
   Then run through MobSF or YARA with stalkerware-specific rules.

MARKETING-STYLE STALKERWARE (Grayware) — WHAT TO LOOK FOR:

These are "legitimate" parental monitoring / employee monitoring apps
that cross into spyware when used without consent:
- mSpy, FlexiSpy, Cocospy, Spyic, TheOneSpy, uMobix, PanSpy,
  Spyzie, Hoverwatch,XnSpy, Mobilespy, MocoSpy, iKeyMonitor,
  LetMeSpy, HighsterMobile, PhoneSheriff, WebWatcher

They typically:
- Require accessibility service
- Hide their launcher icon (or use a fake calculator/utility icon)
- Register as device admin
- Request excessive permissions (SMS, contacts, location, camera, mic)
- Have C2 / panel domains for the "monitoring" dashboard
- Can be identified by their package names and domains in the
  stalkerware-indicators list

================================================================================
SECTION 5 — NOTABLE RECENT ANDROID MALWARE FAMILIES (2025-2026)
================================================================================

This section covers specific malware families and campaigns identified in
2025-2026 research. For attribution methodology, see the companion file
android-malware-attribution-campaign-tracking.md (Section 12 has expanded
case studies). These are family-level descriptions for detection context.

### 5.1 ClayRat — Self-Propagating Spyware (Oct-Dec 2025)

- **Who:** Identified by Zimperium, Oct 2025
- **Target:** Russian users
- **Scale:** 700+ unique APKs in 3 months
- **Distribution:** 25+ phishing domains, Dropbox, Telegram, SMS self-propagation
- **Capabilities:**
  - Original: Default SMS handler abuse, SMS/call log/notification theft,
    front-camera photos, mass SMS to contacts, remote commands
  - Upgraded (v3.0.8, Dec 2025): Accessibility Services added — keylogger
    (PIN/password/pattern + auto-unlock), screen recording (MediaProjection),
    fake overlays (system update/battery/PIN capture), fake notification
    reply intercept, anti-power-down/uninstall button tapping
- **Technical:** AES/CBC encrypted payload in assets; alternate variant uses
  AES-GCM C2 + dynamic payload loading; HTTP C2 with "apezdolskynet" marker
  in Base64 payloads
- **Detection angles:** SMS default handler abuse, Accessibility Services
  enrollment, contact-list SMS propagation, anti-uninstall behavior, known
  phishing domains
- **Source:** Zimperium + SecurityAffairs

### 5.2 Arsink RAT — Cloud-Native Mass Spyware (2025-2026)

- **Who:** Identified by Zimperium zLabs, 2025
- **Scale:** 1,216 APKs, 45K IPs, 143 countries, 4 variants
- **Target concentrations:** Egypt (~13K), Indonesia (~7K), Iraq (~3K),
  Yemen (~3K), Turkey (~2K)
- **Distribution:** Telegram, Discord, MediaFire, impersonating 50+ brands
- **Capabilities:** Device snapshot + identifiers (IP, model, OS, battery,
  location, Google emails), SMS harvesting + continuous exfil, call logs,
  contacts, microphone recordings, photos + files, remote control (flashlight,
  vibration, audio, wallpaper, TTS, CALL_PHONE, file ops, wipe)
- **C2:** Google Apps Script + Firebase RTDB (317 endpoints) + Firebase
  Storage + Google Drive + Telegram. Cloud-native — abuses legitimate Google
  infrastructure rather than dedicated C2 servers.
- **Takedown:** Zimperium + Google dismantled Firebase endpoints and Apps
  Script instances. Multi-infrastructure redundancy across 4 variants.
- **Detection angles:** Firebase/Apps Script C2 traffic, Google Drive
  exfiltration patterns, Telegram Bot API C2, known brand-impersonation
  packages
- **Source:** Zimperium (the-rise-of-arsink-rat)

### 5.3 PromptSpy — AI-Powered Malware (Feb 2026)

- **Who:** ESET Research, Feb 10 2026 (variant); VNCSpy Jan 13 2026 (HK)
- **What:** First known Android malware using generative AI (Google Gemini)
  in execution flow — for persistence
- **Mechanism:** Sends Gemini NL prompt + XML screen dump → Gemini returns
  JSON tap-coordinate instructions → keeps app locked in recent apps list.
  Feedback loop confirms with Gemini until AI validates lock. Replaces
  hardcoded UI selectors with dynamic, device-agnostic AI-guided behavior.
- **Distribution:** Fake Chase Bank (JPMorgan Argentina) → MorganArg dropper
  → PromptSpy payload
- **Other capabilities:** VNC module, lockscreen data capture, uninstall
  blocking, screenshots, screen video recording
- **Status:** Not in ESET telemetry — possibly PoC. Distribution domain
  exists → real targeting (Argentina)
- **Why it matters:** New evasion paradigm. AI-guided persistence beats
  hardcoded selectors across devices/OS versions/manufacturer skins. Signals
  direction for future malware: GenAI APIs at runtime for adaptive behavior.
- **Source:** ESET Research (welivesecurity.com)

### 5.4 Albiriox — MaaS Banking RAT (Dec 2025)

- **Who:** Malwarebytes, Dec 2025
- **Model:** Malware-as-a-Service — RAT + banking Trojan sold to criminals
- **First seen:** Sep 2025, recruitment phase
- **Target:** 400+ banking/crypto/fintech apps. Started in Austria, expanding
  globally.
- **Capabilities:** Live screen streaming to attacker (real-time tap/swipe/
  type), on-device fraud via victim's own banking session, Accessibility
  Services abuse, overlay attacks (in dev), black-screen masking
- **Why it matters:** MaaS commodifies sophisticated banking fraud. Live
  remote control + on-device fraud bypasses MFA and device fingerprinting.
- **Source:** Malwarebytes

### 5.5 Zimperium 4 Campaigns — RecruitRat, SaferRat, Astrinox/Mirax, Massiv

- **Source:** Zimperium Banking Heist Report / zLabs
- **Scale:** 800+ targeted apps (banking, crypto, social media)
- **Common TTPs:**
  - Multi-stage installation with Session Installation API abuse
  - Droppers disguised as Google Play Store updates
  - Non-interactive overlays during permission grants
  - Structural APK tampering
  - Overlay attacks, fake lock screens
  - Package discovery without QUERY_ALL_PACKAGES
  - Near-zero signature detection
- **Individual:**
  - RecruitRat: fake recruitment/job platforms
  - SaferRat: fake premium streaming sites
  - Astrinox/Mirax/Medusa: mimics HireX via xhire[.]cc; also Cleafy's Medusa
  - Massiv: distribution infrastructure unidentified
- **Source:** Zimperium, Cleafy Labs

### 5.6 NFC Relay Fraud Evolution (2025-2026)

- **NGate (2024-2025):** Built on NFCGate (TU Darmstadt). Relays EMV APDUs
  + PIN to attacker device → ATM cash withdrawal. ESET reported 87% increase
  in NFC threats H2 2025 vs H2 2024.
- **Ghost Tap (2025):** Scaled NFC relay deployment, global NFC payment apps.
- **WindRelay + SpyNote (Aug 2026):** Latest evolution. 13-minute phone call
  attack chain: social engineering → SpyNote install → remote access →
  WindRelay install → card tap + PIN → NFC relay → loan fraud + card cloning.
  Targets: Czechia, Slovakia, Slovenia. ~24 samples on VT, Nov 2025-Jul 2026.
- **Pattern:** Social engineering + device takeover + real-time NFC relay.
  Combines spyware (SpyNote) with card cloning (WindRelay/NFCGate) in single
  seamless attack.
- **Sources:** Group-IB, BleepingComputer, TechRadar, Malwarebytes

================================================================================
APPENDIX — QUICK COMMAND REFERENCE CARD
================================================================================

ENUMERATE:
  adb shell pm list packages -3                          # all 3rd-party
  adb shell pm list packages -s                          # all system
  adb shell pm list packages -3 | grep -iE "spy|track"  # stalkerware grep
  adb shell dumpsys package <pkg>                        # full component dump
  adb shell pm path <pkg>                                # APK location
  adb shell ps -A | grep <pkg>                           # running processes
  adb shell settings get secure enabled_accessibility_services  # accessibility
  adb shell dumpsys device_policy                        # device admin/owner

PULL APKS:
  adb shell pm path <pkg> | cut -d: -f2 | xargs -I{} adb pull {} /tmp/

ANALYZE APK (offline):
  apktool d app.apk -o extracted/                        # decode resources + smali
  jadx app.apk                                           # decompile to Java
  androguard app.apk                                     # programmatic analysis
  yara -r rules/ extracted/                              # YARA scan
  mobsf scan app.apk                                     # full MobSF analysis

DYNAMIC (root/jailbreak needed):
  frida -U -l hook.js -f <package>                      # Frida instrumentation
  frida-ps -U                                            # list running processes

IOC SCANNING:
  mvt-android check-device --iocs iocs.json             # live device
  mvt-android check-backup --iocs iocs.json backup.tar  # backup

KEY FILES TO CHECK ON DEVICE:
  /data/local/tmp/                                       # dropped payloads
  /sdcard/Download/                                      # downloaded payloads
  /data/data/<pkg>/                                      # app private data
  /sdcard/Android/data/<pkg>/                           # scoped storage
  /data/system/packages.list                             # package list
  /data/system/packages.xml                              # full package manifest

================================================================================
END
================================================================================
```

`android-malware-detection-tools-survey.md`:

```md
# Android Malware Detection, Stealthy-Behavior Hunting & Forensic Analysis — GitHub Tool Survey

**Search conducted:** 2026-09-04  
**Scope:** GitHub repos for Android malware detection, stealth/hiding behavior hunting, forensic analysis, YARA rules, static/dynamic analysis scripts, exploit identification.

---

## 1. Primary Detection Tools

### [Krypteria/Yaralyze](https://github.com/Krypteria/Yaralyze) — 22 ★
**What it is:** Android malware detection tool using two static analysis techniques: YARA rule scanning + hash-based lookup.  
**Detects:** Known malware families — Brata, Sharkbot, Cerberus, Flubot (validated in testing).  
**Methodology:** Client-server architecture. Server hosts 130,000+ YARA rules and 500,000+ malware hashes sourced from VirusShare and GitHub. Client uploads APK hash; server runs full YARA scan. No dynamic analysis. Rules and hashes are not published in the repo.  
**Stealth notes:** Pure signature/hash matching — won't catch novel or heavily obfuscated variants.

---

### [anpa1200/Android-Malware-Analysis](https://github.com/anpa1200/Android-Malware-Analysis) — 4 ★
**What it is:** Terminal APK static-analysis framework. Triages Android malware into YARA hits, ATT&CK mappings, Frida hooks, and IOC context.  
**Detects:** 20 built-in YARA rules covering Anubis, Cerberus, CryDroid ransomware, Joker, Metasploit RAT, Spyware MultiVector, SMSStealer, Stalkerware, Dropper, Evasion.  
**Methodology:**
- `androguard` static analysis → YARA rules → semantic component scoring (e.g., names like "ServiceRAT" trigger RAT capability scoring)
- Shannon entropy detection for encrypted payloads
- MITRE ATT&CK for Mobile mapping
- Auto-generated Frida instrumentation scripts for dynamic follow-up
- VirusTotal API v3 integration (optional)
- Multi-LLM analysis (Claude, OpenAI GPT-4o, Gemini, Ollama) via `--provider` flag
- JSON + terminal UI reports
- Validated on 12 real malware samples: 11/12 correct classification  
**Stealth notes:** Semantic component-name scoring helps catch obfuscated/misnamed components. Frida hooks generated automatically for dynamic confirmation. Entropy detection flags encrypted payloads (common in stealth loaders).

---

### [MobSF/Mobile-Security-Framework-MobSF](https://github.com/MobSF/Mobile-Security-Framework-MobSF) — 21.7k ★
**What it is:** The most popular all-in-one mobile app security assessment framework. Automated static AND dynamic analysis for Android, iOS, and Windows apps.  
**Detects:** Vast range — malware patterns, security vulnerabilities, privacy issues, Manifest permission analysis, code injection, insecure cryptography, hardcoded secrets, component exposure, and more.  
**Methodology:** Fat framework. Static analysis via APK decompilation, Manifest parsing, certificate analysis, strings extraction, and pattern matching. Dynamic analysis via instrumented emulator/device. Integrates multiple engines. Web UI + CLI (via `mobsfscan`).  
**Stealth notes:** Dynamic analysis component can catch behaviors hidden from static inspection. Has specific checks for insecure component exports, intent filters, and debuggable builds — common stealth misconfigurations.

---

### [momenbasel/malware-check](https://github.com/momenbasel/malware-check) — 64 ★
**What it is:** Static + dynamic analysis tool for detecting malicious code, suspicious binaries, and privacy violations across many formats (APK, IPA, PE, ELF, source code).  
**Detects:** Reverse shells, backdoors, web shells, obfuscated payloads, crypto miners, ransomware patterns, keyloggers, credential theft, supply chain attacks, persistence mechanisms, anti-analysis (debugger/VM detection), tracking SDKs, PII field handling, invasive permissions, device fingerprinting.  
**Methodology:**
- Static: source code scanner (15+ languages), binary analyzer (PE/Mach-O/ELF with entropy detection, import table inspection, RWX section detection), YARA engine with bundled rules, privacy analyzer
- Dynamic: Docker sandbox with `strace` syscall tracing, `tcpdump` network monitoring, filesystem monitoring, process monitoring. Full isolation: no network, memory limits, dropped capabilities, read-only rootfs
- Reporting: console, JSON, HTML dashboard, SARIF 2.1.0 (GitHub Code Scanning / Azure DevOps / GitLab integration)
- MITRE ATT&CK coverage mapped (T1059, T1505.003, T1027, T1496, T1486, T1056.001, T1053, T1547, T1055, T1555, T1548, T1622, T1195)  
**Stealth notes:** Docker behavioral sandbox catches runtime behaviors static analysis misses. Anti-analysis detection (debugger/VM checks) is explicitly modeled. Network monitoring catches C2 beaconing that static analysis can't see.

---

### [maaaaz/androwarn](https://github.com/maaaaz/androwarn) — 532 ★
**What it is:** Static code analyzer for malicious Android applications. Analyzes Dalvik bytecode (Smali) via `androguard`.  
**Detects (categories):**
- Telephony identifiers exfiltration (IMEI, IMSI, MCC, MNC, LAC, CID, operator name)
- Device settings exfiltration (software version, usage stats, system settings, logs)
- Geolocation leakage (GPS/WiFi)
- Connection interfaces exfiltration (WiFi credentials, Bluetooth MAC)
- Telephony services abuse (premium SMS, phone call composition)
- Audio/video flow interception (call recording, video capture)
- Remote connection establishment (socket open, Bluetooth pairing, APN edit)
- PIM data leakage (contacts, calendar, SMS, mail, clipboard)
- External memory operations (SD card file access)
- PIM data modification (add/delete contacts, calendar events)
- Arbitrary code execution (JNI native code, UNIX commands, privilege escalation)
- Denial of Service (event notification deactivation, file deletion, process killing, keyboard disable, shutdown/reboot)  
**Methodology:** Three verbosity levels (Essential/Advanced/Expert). Output: plaintext, HTML (Bootstrap), JSON.  
**Stealth notes:** Data-flow analysis on Smali catches exfiltration paths even when the code is obfuscated — it tracks API usage patterns rather than string signatures. However, last commit was 2019; may miss newer API surfaces.

---

### [quark-engine/quark-engine](https://github.com/ev-flow/quark-engine) — 1.7k ★
**What it is:** Obfuscation-neglect Android malware scoring system.  
**Detects:** Malicious behavior patterns independent of obfuscation. Active development (v26.9.1, last commit 19 hours ago at time of search).  
**Methodology:** Scores APK behavior rather than matching signatures. Has a Claude Code plugin (`/quark` and `/quark-rule-gen` skills) for AI-driven analysis and rule generation. Rule validation script against real APKs.  
**Stealth notes:** Designed explicitly to work *despite* obfuscation — scores behavioral patterns rather than relying on string signatures that obfuscation breaks.

---

### [dwisiswant0/apkleaks](https://github.com/dwisiswant0/apkleaks) — 6.3k ★
**What it is:** Scans APK files for URIs, endpoints, and secrets via regex patterns on decompiled code.  
**Detects:** Hard-coded API keys, AWS credentials, URLs/endpoints, tokens, secrets embedded in APKs.  
**Methodology:** Uses `jadx` decompiler + configurable regex patterns. Custom pattern files supported. Docker image available.  
**Stealth notes:** Catches hardcoded C2 URLs, API keys, and credentials that stealthy malware often embeds — even when code is obfuscated, strings used at runtime often survive in the APK.

---

## 2. YARA Rules Repositories

### [Yara-Rules/rules](https://github.com/Yara-Rules/rules) — 4.9k ★
**What it is:** Community-compiled YARA rules repository.  
**Contents:** folders for `malware`, `mobile_malware` (noted as deprecated in indexing — Androguard-based mobile rules outdated), `exploit_kits`, `cve_rules`, `packers`, `antidebug_antivm`, `capabilities`, `webshells`, `maldocs`, `email`. Indexed `.yar` files per category.  
**Stealth notes:** `antidebug_antivm` rules detect anti-analysis; `packers` rules detect packed/obfuscated binaries; `exploit_kits` for exploit detection.

---

### [Neo23x0/signature-base](https://github.com/Neo23x0/signature-base) — 3k ★
**What it is:** Florian Roth's high-quality YARA signature and IOC database. Used by LOKI and THOR scanners. Actively maintained (commit 3 days ago).  
**Detects:** High-quality, low-false-positive YARA rules for malware families, IOC hash sets, generic anomalies, cloaking/obfuscation patterns, webshells, APT-related indicators.  
**Methodology:** Rigorous quality control — CI syntax checking on every push/PR. DRL 1.1 license. Commercial feed available with 20× more rules.  
**Stealth notes:** `general_cloaking` and `generic_anomalies` rules specifically target evasion/hiding patterns. Rules are engineered for minimal false positives, important when scanning large APK corpora.

---

### [YARA Forge](https://yarahq.github.io/) — community project
**What it is:** Curated, quality-filtered YARA rule collection assembled from multiple community repos (Elastic, etc.) with quality scoring.  
**Stealth notes:** Aggregates rules from multiple sources with automated quality filtering — good starting point for a comprehensive YARA ruleset.

---

### [citizenlab/malware-signatures](https://github.com/citizenlab/malware-signatures) — 
**What it is:** YARA signatures from Citizen Lab's research on targeted attacks against civil society organizations.  
**Stealth notes:** Focused on sophisticated, targeted spyware (Pegasus-family and similar). Signatures derived from real-world targeted attack investigations — high-signal, low-noise rules for advanced threats.

---

## 3. Forensic Analysis Tools

### [mvt-project/androidqf](https://github.com/mvt-project/androidqf) — 261 ★
**What it is:** Android Quick Forensics — portable tool for acquiring forensic evidence from Android devices to identify traces of compromise. Outputs feeds into MVT (Mobile Verification Toolkit).  
**Detects:** Traces of compromise on Android devices — relevant for spyware/stalkerware triage (Pegasus-style threats). Collects: installed apps, APK archives, app data, logs, browser history, Magisk modules, and more.  
**Methodology:**
- Go-based, single binary, ADB-dependent
- Streams acquisitions to zip archives
- Supports age encryption for consensual forensics (operator can't decrypt even under duress)
- Designed for *consensual* forensic analysis — licensing explicitly prohibits adversarial use
- Latest commit: 3 days ago (active)  
**Stealth notes:** Targets post-compromise forensics — finding what's already on the device. Works at the OS level (not APK analysis), so it can find installed malware that's actively hiding its icon or running as a background service.

---

### [AvillaDaniel/AvillaForensics](https://github.com/AvillaDaniel/AvillaForensics) — 
**What it is:** Mobile forensics toolkit covering Android/iOS/Instagram/Telegram/WhatsApp/Signal.  
**Detects:** WhatsApp parsers, Signal analysis, APK analysis via apktool, downgrade attack forensics, ADB command collection.  
**Stealth notes:** App-specific forensics (WhatsApp, Signal) can uncover spyware that hooks or monitors messaging apps.

---

### Mesquidar/ForensicsTools (curated list) — 
**What it is:** Curated list of free/open forensics tools.  
**Notable entries for Android:** Andriller (read-only forensic acquisition from Android devices), MobSF, and references to mobile forensics tools.

---

## 4. Stealthy-Behavior & Hiding Methodology Resources

### [Diesoi/AndoidStegoLoader](https://github.com/Diesoi/AndoidStegoLoader) — archived (2025)
**What it is:** Research repository for the paper *"Analysis and Detection of Android Stegomalware: the Impact of the Loading Stage"* (IH&MMSec 2025).  
**Methodology:**
- Stegomalware hides payload inside images (steganography) → decodes at runtime → loads malicious code dynamically
- Contains source code of a custom-built stegomalware application + smali code of loader classes
- Detection methodology: static analysis of the loader component + memory forensics (fridump) to capture decoded payloads in RAM
- Memory dump workflow: rooted device → `frida-server` → `fridump -U AppName -o out_dir -s` → analyze strings in dump for decoded URLs, IPs, image magic numbers
- VirusTotal results included for the testbed APKs  
**Key finding:** The *loading stage* is the critical detection point — stegomalware that successfully decodes and loads at runtime evades static analysis; memory forensics at runtime catches the decoded payload.

---

### [tthtlc/awesome_malware_techniques](https://github.com/tthtlc/awesome_malware_techniques) — 29 ★
**What it is:** Curated bibliography of malware techniques research, with a dedicated `android_malware_technique.md` file.  
**Contents:** 600+ references covering Android malware techniques, detection methods, obfuscation, evasion, collusion attacks, inter-app communication threats, energy attacks, kernel rootkits, trusted detection, adversarial ML attacks on malware classifiers, and more.  
**Stealth notes:** Academic paper compendium — covers stegomalware, dynamic code loading, obfuscation resilience, adversarial examples against ML detectors, repackaging evasion, hidden sensitive operations (Dark Hazard, NDSS 2017), component hijacking (CHEX, CCS 2012).

---

### [lucacav/steg-in-the-wild](https://github.com/lucav/steg-in-the-wild) — 
**What it is:** List of real-world attacks and malware that use steganography.  
**Notable:** Android/Twitoor (encrypted commands retrieved from steganographic images), references to "Never Mind the Malware, Here's the Stegomalware" (IEEE S&P).

---

### [Ocram95/AndroidStego](https://github.com/Ocram95/AndroidStego) — 
**What it is:** Research on feasibility of Android stegomalware detection. Paper repo: *"On the Feasibility of Android Stegomalware: a Detection Study"* (Italian Conference on Cybersecurity).

---

### Reza-saeedi/Malware-Detection-Tools — 
**Notable entries for stealthy behavior:**
- **InputScope** (OSU Sec Lab): Static analysis to uncover *hidden behaviors from user-input validation* in mobile apps. Paper at SP 2020.
- **StaDynA**: System for analyzing apps with *dynamic code update features* (dynamic class loading + reflection). Combines static + dynamic analysis to reveal hidden/updated behavior that static analysis alone misses. This directly addresses the "disabled components / dynamic loading" stealth pattern.
- **truegaze**: Static analysis for Android/iOS focusing on security issues outside source code.

---

### [ashishb/android-security-awesome](https://github.com/ashishb/android-security-awesome) — 
**What it is:** Massive curated list of Android security tools with 600+ entries.  
**Key stealth-relevant entries:**
- **Inspeckage** (Xposed module): Dynamic analysis with API hooks, can start unexported activities — directly useful for activating hidden components
- **FriTap**: Intercept SSL/TLS with Frida, extract TLS keys as PCAP — catches encrypted C2 that static analysis can't read
- **Runtime Mobile Security (RMS)**: Web interface for manipulating Android/iOS apps at runtime
- **Brida**: Burp Suite + Frida bridge — manipulate app methods while tampering with backend traffic
- **FlutterTap**: Zygisk module bypassing BoringSSL cert verification in Flutter apps — catches Flutter-based malware that traditional Frida SSL unpinning misses
- **Androl4b**: VM for Android RE and malware analysis
- **Android Malware Genome Project**: 1,260 samples across 49 families (research)

---

## 5. Dynamic Analysis & Sandboxing

### [pjlantz/droidbox](https://github.com/pjlantz/droidbox) — 
**What it is:** Dynamic analysis of Android apps in a sandbox.  
**Detects:** File system changes, network activity, SMS/phone usage, contact access, device info access, and more during runtime execution.

---

### [alexMyG/AndroPyTool](https://github.com/alexMyG/AndroPyTool) — 
**What it is:** Framework for automated extraction of static and dynamic features from Android APKs.  
**Methodology:** Combines DroidBox, FlowDroid, Strace, AndroGuard, and VirusTotal analysis into one pipeline. Has an associated paper.

---

### [zyrikby/StaDynA](https://github.com/zyrikby/StaDynA) — 
**What it is:** System for analyzing Android apps that use dynamic code updates (DexClassLoader, reflection).  
**Stealth relevance:** This is the key tool for detecting malware that *hides behavior behind dynamic code loading*. Static analysis alone sees only the loader stub; StaDynA combines static+dynamic to reveal the actually-loaded code's behavior.

---

### [CuckooDroid](https://github.com/idanr1986/cuckoo-droid) — 
**What it is:** Android extension for Cuckoo sandbox. Automated dynamic analysis of Android APKs in an isolated environment.

---

### [Inspeckage](https://github.com/ac-pm/Inspeckage) — 
**What it is:** Android Package Inspector — Xposed module for dynamic analysis.  
**Capability:** Hooks API calls, starts unexported activities/services/receivers, logs runtime behavior. Directly useful for "waking up" disabled or hidden components for inspection.

## 8. How Stealthy Android Malware Hides — Methodology Summary

From the collected repos and papers, here are the primary hiding techniques and how to counter them:

| Hiding Technique | How It Works | Detection Approach | Tools |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Steganographic payloads** | Malicious code hidden in images; decoded at runtime via steganography lib | Memory forensics (fridump) at runtime to capture decoded payload; static analysis of decoder stub | AndoidStegoLoader (research), fridump, MobSF dynamic |
| **Dynamic code loading** | DexClassLoader / Runtime.exec / reflection loads code not present in initial APK | StaDynA (static+dynamic combination), Frida hooks on DexClassLoader, entropy detection on loaded DEX | StaDynA, Android-Malware-Analysis (Frida hooks), androwarn |
| **Disabled components** | Receiver/service/activity declared `android:enabled="false"` in Manifest, activated later via hidden intent | Inspeckage (starts unexported components), static Manifest analysis, component permission analysis | Inspeckage, androwarn, MobSF Manifest checks |
| **Obfuscation (ProGuard/R8/string encryption)** | Renamed classes/methods, encrypted strings, control-flow flattening | Obfuscation-neglect scoring (Quark Engine), semantic naming analysis (ServiceRAT → RAT), entropy analysis, YARA on Smali patterns | Quark Engine, Android-Malware-Analysis, yarGen-generated rules |
```
