`shizuku-adb-workflows.md`:

```md
================================================================================
ADB + Shizuku Operational Workflows for Android Security Work
Research summary by Solar Pro4 — 2026-09-04
================================================================================

SECTION 1 — MOST USEFUL TOOL REPOS
================================================================================

1. RikkaApps/Shizuku (29.7k stars, Apache 2.0)
   https://github.com/RikkaApps/Shizuku
   The foundational privileged-process server. Starts a Java process via
   app_process with ADB/shell UID (2000) or root UID (0), exposes selected
   Android system APIs over Binder. This is the core infrastructure every
   other Shizuku-based tool builds on. Without it, none of the downstream
   apps or workflows function.

2. timschneeb/awesome-shizuku (10k stars, CC BY-SA 3.0)
   https://github.com/timschneeb/awesome-shizuku
   Curated catalog of Shizuku-using apps organized by category (terminals,
   file managers, debloaters, device-owner tools, automation flows).
   Essential for finding the right tool for a specific task. Also lists
   Automate flows for keeping Shizuku alive, and the rish shell client.

3. iamr0s/Dhizuku (3.8k stars, GPL-3.0)
   https://github.com/iamr0s/Dhizuku
   Shares Device Owner (DPM) permissions to third-party apps via Binder.
   Different privilege axis from Shizuku — grants uninstall, install,
   verify, and managed-profile powers rather than shell UID. Android allows
   only ONE device owner, so Dhizuku and Shizuku are complementary, not
   drop-in replacements.

4. ahmed-alnassif/AndroSH (240 stars, GPL-3.0)
   https://github.com/ahmed-alnassif/AndroSH
   No-root multi-distro Linux (Arch, Fedora, Alpine, Debian, Ubuntu, Kali,
   Void, Manjaro, openSUSE, Chimera) running via Shizuku/ADB + proot.
   Bundles Termux:X11 GUI. Useful when you need actual Linux tooling (binwalk,
   strings, foremost, tcpdump) on a non-rooted device for forensics or pentest
   workloads.

5. thejaustin/ShizukuPlus (fork, Samsung UID 1000 exploit)
   https://github.com/thejaustin/ShizukuPlus
   Enhanced Shizuku fork that adds a Samsung-specific UID 1000 system
   execution path on top of the standard ADB/shell mode. Relevant when
   working on Samsung devices where the vendor exploit gives you more than
   shell but less than full root.

6. RikkaApps/Shizuku-API (official API library)
   https://github.com/RikkaApps/Shizuku-API
   The developer library apps link against. Provides ShizukuBinderWrapper,
   UserService binding, permission grant flow, and UID detection. Any
   custom tooling or in-house audit app you build will use this.

7. DP-Hridayan/aShellYou (Shizuku-based terminal)
   https://github.com/DP-Hridayan/aShellYou
   Terminal app that uses Shizuku to execute shell commands locally on the
   device without PC or USB. Practical for on-device incident response when
   you can't connect a workstation.

8. HackTricks Shizuku page (reference methodology)
   https://hacktricks.wiki/en/mobile-pentesting/android-app-pentesting/shizuku-privileged-api.html
   Not a repo, but the best consolidated writeup on Shizuku architecture
   (Binder wrapper, UserService, shell vs root UID, security implications,
   detection ideas, abuse chains). Worth reading alongside the code.

---

SECTION 2 — CONCRETE COMMAND / WORKFLOW EXAMPLES
================================================================================

A. DEVICE ACCESS — starting and verifying Shizuku
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 1. Start Shizuku via USB ADB (non-root)
adb shell sh /sdcard/Android/data/moe.shizuku.privileged.api/start.sh

# 2. Start Shizuku via Wireless Debugging (Android 11+, no USB)
#    First pair once: Settings > Developer Options > Wireless debugging > Pair device
#    Then in Shizuku app: "Start via Wireless debugging"
adb connect <DEVICE_IP>:5555
adb shell sh /sdcard/Android/data/moe.shizuku.privileged.api/start.sh

# 3. Start Shizuku via root (Magisk/KernelSU, persists across reboot as daemon)
su -c sh /data/adb/shizuku/start.sh

# 4. Verify Shizuku is running
adb shell dumpsys activity service moe.shizuku.privileged.api | head
adb shell service list | grep shizuku
# Expect to see moe.shizuku.privileged.api in the service list

# 5. Check effective privilege tier from a Shizuku-enabled app / rish shell
#    Shizuku.getUid() returns 2000 (shell) or 0 (root)
adb shell sh /sdcard/Android/data/moe.shizuku.privileged.api/start.sh
#    then open rish shell via Shizuku app "Use Shizuku in terminal apps"

# 6. Restart ADB server if Shizuku won't attach (stale auth)
adb kill-server && adb start-server
adb shell sh /sdcard/Android/data/moe.shizuku.privileged.api/start.sh

B. APP INTERROGATION — package, permission, and component enumeration
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 7. List all packages (shell-equivalent via Shizuku proxy)
adb shell pm list packages
adb shell pm list packages -3                  # third-party only
adb shell pm list packages -s                  # system only
adb shell pm list packages -f                  # with APK path
adb shell pm list packages | grep -i camera    # filter by keyword

# 8. Inspect a single package in depth
adb shell dumpsys package com.example.target
#    Look for: userId, declared permissions, intents, activities, providers,
#    receivers, services, signatures, installLocation, sharedUserId

# 9. Find the UID and GID for a package
adb shell dumpsys package com.example.target | grep -E 'userId|gid'

# 10. Enumerate app permissions and compare granted vs requested
adb shell dumpsys package com.example.target | grep -A20 'granted=true'
adb shell dumpsys package com.example.target | grep permission

# 11. Grant / revoke permissions (works via Shizuku shell, no root needed)
adb shell pm grant com.example.target android.permission.READ_CONTACTS
adb shell pm revoke com.example.target android.permission.READ_CONTACTS
adb shell pm reset-permissions                  # reset all to defaults

# 12. Enable / disable (debloat) system and user apps
adb shell pm disable --user 0 com.google.android.apps.tips
adb shell pm enable com.google.android.apps.tips
adb shell pm uninstall --user 0 com.bloat.package   # remove for current user

# 13. Launch an activity or component for interaction testing
adb shell am start -n com.example.target/.MainActivity
adb shell am start -a com.example.target.SOME_ACTION -d "https://example.com"

# 14. Dump activity service state for a package
adb shell dumpsys activity services com.example.target
adb shell dumpsys activity providers com.example.target
adb shell dumpsys activity broadcasts              # recent broadcast history

# 15. Inspect app ops (granular runtime permission behavior)
adb shell appops get com.example.target
adb shell appops set com.example.target READ_CONTACTS deny
adb shell appops set com.example.target READ_CONTACTS allow

C. FILE SYSTEM FORENSICS — what shell can and cannot reach
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

NOTE: Shell UID (2000) CANNOT read /data/user/0/<package> private sandbox
directly. You get: /sdcard (shared storage), app cache dirs you own,
Android/data/<package> (Android 11+ scoped storage visibility), and world-
readable paths. Root unlocks everything below /data.

# 16. Pull shared storage for triage
adb pull /sdcard/Download/ ./evidence/Download/
adb pull /sdcard/Pictures/ ./evidence/Pictures/
adb pull /sdcard/DCIM/ ./evidence/DCIM/

# 17. List Android/data contents (visible since Android 11 to shell)
adb shell ls -la /sdcard/Android/data/
adb shell ls -la /sdcard/Android/data/com.example.target/
adb shell ls -la /sdcard/Android/obb/

# 18. Pull known APK path from pm list packages -f
adb shell pm path com.example.target
#    Returns: package:/data/app/~~/base.apk
adb pull /data/app/~~/com.example.target-==/base.apk ./evidence/

# 19. Copy app cache/logs if permissions allow (depends on OEM)
adb shell ls -la /data/local/tmp/
adb shell cat /data/local/tmp/some_shared_log  # if world-readable

# 20. AndroSH: drop into a Kali/Ubuntu proot for Linux forensics tooling
#    Prerequisite: Shizuku running, AndroSH installed in Termux
cd ~/AndroSH && python main.py launch <env_name>
#    Then inside the proot:
binwalk image.jpg
strings -n 8 suspicious.apk
foremost -i evidence.img -o output/
tcpdump -i any -w capture.pcap              # if network interface accessible

D. LOG COLLECTION — live and post-incident
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 21. Dump full logcat to file (live device)
adb logcat -d > logs/logcat_$(date +%Y%m%d_%H%M%S).txt

# 22. Filter logcat by tag, PID, or priority
adb logcat -d -s MainActivity              # specific tag
adb logcat -d | grep -i "com.example.target"
adb logcat -d -b all                       # all buffers (main, system, crash, radio)

# 23. Live tail for interactive monitoring
adb logcat | grep -E "FATAL|Exception|com.example.target"
adb logcat -v threadtime                    # high-resolution timestamps

# 24. Full bugreport (heavy, captures logs + dumpsys + traces + last_kmsg)
adb bugreport > evidence/bugreport_$(date +%Y%m%d).zip

# 25. Targeted dumpsys for specific subsystems
adb shell dumpsys batterystats --checkin
adb shell dumpsys meminfo com.example.target
adb shell dumpsys netstats
adb shell dumpsys connectivity
adb shell dumpsys window windows            # view hierarchy, focused window

# 26. Collect crash traces
adb shell cat /data/system/dropbox/        # if accessible (often restricted)
adb logcat -d -b crash > evidence/crash.txt
adb shell dumpsys dropbox                  # dropbox manager state

E. SHIZUKU-SPECIFIC TERMINAL WORKFLOW (rish shell, no PC)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 27. Enable rish shell in Shizuku app: Settings > "Use Shizuku in terminal apps"
#     Shizuku downloads rish binary. Then from a terminal app on-device:

shizuku shell                               # interactive shell with shell UID 2000
shizuku exec "pm list packages -3"         # single command
shizuku open                                # launch Shizuku app GUI

# 28. Termux + Shizuku integration (no PC at all)
#     In Termux:
pkg install wget
wget https://rikka.app/rish/latest -O rish && chmod +x rish
#     Then use rish or the Shizuku Termux integration to run shell commands
#     directly on-device with ADB-equivalent privileges.

F. ADB BACKUP / RESTORE (legacy, limited Android 12+)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 29. Backup a single app with APK (works best on Android 9-11; limited post-12)
adb shell pm list packages -f -3
adb backup -f evidence/com.example.target.ab -apk com.example.target
adb restore evidence/com.example.target.ab

# 30. Full device backup (noisy, often restricted on modern Android)
adb backup -apk -shared -system -all -f evidence/full_device.ab

---

SECTION 3 — SHIZUKU vs DHIZUKU vs ROOT, AND PRIVILEGE TIERS
================================================================================

A. THE THREE MODES OF ELEVATED ACCESS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

SHIZUKU (ADB/shell mode, UID 2000):
  - How: User enables USB or Wireless debugging, Shizuku app runs
    start.sh via adbd, which launches a Java process via app_process
    running as the shell user (uid=2000).
  - What you get: Everything adb shell can do — pm, dumpsys, am, appops,
    settings (some), cmd connectivity, logcat, shared storage access.
    Binder proxy: Shizuku receives requests from authorized apps, forwards
    them to system services, returns results. The app sees the same Binder
    interface as if it were calling the system service directly.
  - What you DON'T get: Reading another app's private data directory
    (/data/user/0/<pkg>), modifying system partitions, loading kernel mods,
    accessing hardware that requires SYSTEM or ROOT uid, bypassing hidden-
    API restrictions from a normal app process (use UserService instead).
  - Persistence: No root = no auto-start at boot. Session dies on reboot
    unless you re-run start.sh. Root mode can install as a daemon at /data/adb/
    so it survives reboot.
  - OEM quirks: MIUI/HyperOS often needs "USB debugging (Security settings)"
    beyond normal USB debugging. ColorOS/OxygenOS may need permission-monitor
    disabled. Android 11+ "Disable adb authorization timeout" helps stability.
  - Detection: service list | grep shizuku, dumpsys activity service
    moe.shizuku.privileged.api. Malware may mimic with its own shell-owned
    Binder helper.

DHIZUKU (Device Owner mode, DPM powers, no UID elevation):
  - How: User wipes device (or uses QR provisioning), makes Dhizuku the
    Device Owner via Android's DevicePolicyManager. Dhizuku then exposes
    Device Owner permissions to other apps via its API.
  - What you get: Install/uninstall apps system-wide, verify apps, manage
    managed profiles, lock task mode, set global/hidden settings, disable
    safe boot. This is a DIFFERENT privilege axis from Shizuku — it's about
    MDM/enterprise control, not shell command execution.
  - What you DON'T get: shell UID, direct command execution, dumpsys, pm
    internals beyond what DPM exposes, file system access beyond normal app.
  - Relationship to Shizuku: Dhizuku's own README notes it can use Shizuku
    as a backend for some operations ("Dhizuku Mode"), and ShizukuPlus lists
    Dhizuku as a component. They are complementary, not competing. Android
    only allows one Device Owner, so you pick Dhizuku or another DO app.
  - Use case: Enterprise device management, kiosk mode, remote app control,
    sideloading without Play Store, managed-profile security testing.

ROOT (UID 0, full Linux root on device):
  - How: Magisk, KernelSU, or kernel exploit. Shizuku can run in root mode
    via `su -c sh /data/adb/shizuku/start.sh`, which gives the Shizuku
    server process UID 0 instead of 2000.
  - What you get: Everything shell can do PLUS: read/write any file
    (including /data/user/0/<pkg>), remount system/vendor partitions,
    load kernel modules, full logcat including kernel logs, ptrace any
    process, access hardware directly, bypass most permission checks.
  - Trade-offs: Detected by SafetyNet/Play Integrity (unless hidden with
    Magisk Hide / Zygisk), wipes on some OTA updates, higher risk of bricking,
    not available on locked bootloader devices without an exploit.

B. SHELL (UID 2000) vs ROOT (UID 0) — PRACTICAL DIFFERENCE TABLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Capability                          Shell (UID 2000)     Root (UID 0)
----------------------------------- --------------------  -------------------
pm list/enable/disable/uninstall    YES (user scope)     YES (system-wide)
dumpsys (most services)             YES                  YES
appops get/set                      YES                  YES
am start/instrument                  YES                  YES
logcat -d                           YES                  YES (all buffers)
pull /sdcard/*                      YES                  YES
pull /data/user/0/<pkg>             NO (sandbox)         YES
pull /system /vendor /product       NO (read-only fs)    YES (if not A/B locked)
install APKs (PackageInstaller)     YES (with Shizuku)   YES (with Shizuku + more flags)
load native libraries / JNI         via UserService only YES (direct)
bypass hidden API restrictions      NO (in app process)  YES (or via UserService)
SELinux                              Enforced             Enforced (unless setenforce 0)
kernel logs (dmesg, pstore)         NO                   YES
ptrace / process injection          NO                   YES

C. KEY PRACTICAL NOTES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

- Shizuku.getUid() tells you which tier you're running. 2000 = shell,
  0 = root. Write tooling to check this and degrade gracefully.

- Even with shell UID, Android permissions, SELinux, and OEM-specific
  restrictions apply. A command that works via `adb shell` on a Pixel may
  fail through Shizuku on a Samsung because the OEM trimmed shell permissions.

- UserService is the modern way to run long-lived Java/JNI code in the
  Shizuku server process. Prefer it over spawning repeated shell commands
  for offensive tooling that needs state or repeated Binder operations.

- Shizuku's Binder wrapper (ShizukuBinderWrapper) makes the app believe it's
  calling the system service directly. The actual transaction runs in the
  Shizuku server process, not the app process. This matters for hidden API
  restrictions — code in the app process is still limited; code in the
  UserService / server process is not.

- Wireless debugging (Android 11+) simplified Shizuku setup but also creates
  a security concern: if an attacker can re-enable Wireless debugging (e.g.,
  via Accessibility + WRITE_SECURE_SETTINGS abuse) and has the ADB pairing
  keys, they can reconnect and recreate a shell session post-boot without
  root. Treat the Accessibility + Wireless debugging + shell-Backed Binder
  chain as a high-risk post-exploitation path.

- For persistence on a non-rooted device, Automate flows exist (Better Shizuku
  Starter, Shizuku Keeper, Shizuku Keeper Lite — listed in awesome-shizuku).
  These watch for Shizuku dying and restart it via wireless debugging on
  events like screen on, boot completed, or periodic checks. Only works if
  the device is already authorized for wireless debugging.

---

SECTION 4 — QUICK-REFERENCE WORKFLOW DECISION TREE
================================================================================

DO YOU HAVE ROOT?
  YES → Shizuku in root mode (`su -c sh /data/adb/shizuku/start.sh`) gives
        UID 0, survives reboot as daemon, can read all /data.
  NO  → Shizuku in ADB/shell mode (UID 2000) gives most pm/dumpsys/am/logcat
        primitives without root.

DO YOU NEED TO READ ANOTHER APP'S PRIVATE DATA?
  YES → Need root, or need to use a Shizuku-enabled file manager that uses
        scoped-storage APIs (e.g., X-plore with Shizuku for Android/data).
        Shell UID cannot read /data/user/0/<pkg> directly.
  NO  → Shell mode is sufficient for most auditing, debloating, and log work.

DO YOU NEED TO INSTALL/UNINSTALL SYSTEM-WIDE OR MANAGE DEVICE POLICY?
  YES → Consider Dhizuku for Device Owner powers (install, uninstall, verify,
        lock task, managed profile). Or get root for full pm control.
  NO  → Shizuku shell for per-user disable/uninstall and permission changes.

DO YOU NEED LINUX TOOLING (binwalk, foremost, tcpdump)?
  YES → AndroSH: Shizuku + proot + Termux:X11 gives you a Kali/Ubuntu/Arch
        environment without root. Good for forensics and pentest on a handset.
  NO  → Standard Shizuku shell + adb pull is enough.

ARE YOU BUILDING CUSTOM TOOLING?
  YES → Use Shizuku-API (ShizukuBinderWrapper + UserService) for Binder-level
        access. Add the Shizuku API permission and ShizukuProvider to your
        manifest. Check Shizuku.getUid() at runtime to know your tier.
  NO  → Use existing Shizuku-enabled apps from awesome-shizuku list.

---

END OF REPORT
================================================================================

```

`shizuku-exploitation-research.md`:

```md
# Shizuku Exploitation Research

**Date:** September 2026  
**Research Scope:** Privilege escalation, abuse scenarios, UserService exploitation, and defensive audit methodology for the Shizuku privilege escalation framework on Android.

---

## 1. Relevant Repositories and Writeups

### Primary Projects

| Project | Description | Stars | Notes |
|---------|-------------|-------|-------|
| **RikkaApps/Shizuku** | Main Shizuku APK providing the privileged Binder server and manager interface | 29.7k | Core framework. Uses `app_process` to start a Java server exposing the `moe.shizuku.privileged.api` Binder service. |
| **RikkaApps/Shizuku-API** | Developer library for building apps that interact with Shizuku's privileged API | — | Official Java/Kotlin library. Apps add this dependency to make Binder calls via Shizuku. |
| **thejaustin/ShizukuPlus** | Unofficial Shizuku client/manager with extended UI and utilities | — | Third-party client showcasing the ecosystem around Shizuku. |
| **awesome-shizuku** | Curated catalog of Shizuku-powered apps | 980+ apps listed | Demonstrates ecosystem breadth — nearly 1000 apps have integrated Shizuku, meaning a wide attack surface if any single app is compromised. |

### Privilege Elevation Methods

| Method | UID | Security Properties |
|--------|-----|---------------------|
| **ADB Shell (default)** | UID 2000 (`shell`) | Requires USB debugging or wireless debugging to be enabled. Grants shell-level access to system APIs. Least privileged legitimate mode. |
| **Sui Magisk Module** | UID 0 (`root`) | Integrates with Magisk's Sui (Superuser Injector). Grants root-level access through Shizuku. Highest privilege tier. |
| **Dhizuku (Device Owner mode)** | Varies | Uses Android Device Owner provisioning to gain elevated privileges. Different security model from ADB/shell mode — relevant for enterprise/MDM contexts. |

### Reference Writeups

- **HackTricks Shizuku page** — Documents the high-risk chain: Accessibility service bypass + Wireless Debugging enabled + shell-backed Binder service = a powerful exploitation primitive. This combination is specifically flagged in offensive security research as a notable attack pathway.
- **Shizuku official documentation** — Discusses the security model, permission grants, and privilege implications. The Shizuku team is aware of the privilege boundary and documents the trust assumptions. Reviewing the official docs is essential for understanding intended vs. unintended usage patterns.

### Known CVEs

**No major public CVEs against Shizuku server code as of the research cutoff.** This does not mean the code is free of vulnerabilities — it means no publicly disclosed, patched CVEs are catalogued. The attack surface (Binder transaction handling, intent filtering, permission checks, UserService setup, Wireless Debugging interaction, update mechanism) remains a live audit target.

---

## 2. Concrete Privilege Escalation and Abuse Scenarios

### Scenario 1: Malicious App Obtains Shizuku Access

**How it works:**

1. An attacker either socially engineers the user into granting Shizuku access to a malicious app, or exploits a vulnerability in a legitimate Shizuku-enabled app to hijack its access token.
2. Once the malicious app holds a Shizuku client connection, it gains shell-grade (UID 2000) or root-grade (UID 0) access to system APIs through the Binder service `moe.shizuku.privileged.api`.
3. The app calls Shizuku-exposed APIs to perform operations that would otherwise be forbidden to normal apps.

**Impact:**

- Read/write data belonging to other apps (if the target app's data directory is accessible at the shell UID level).
- Modify system settings (Settings.Provider, secure settings).
- Manage packages — install, uninstall, disable, or change permissions of other apps.
- Access protected APIs normally gated behind signature|privileged permissions.
- In root mode (Sui): full device compromise including reading any app's data, modifying system partitions, persisting across factory resets (if combined with other persistence mechanisms).

**Why it's possible:**

Shizuku's entire value proposition is providing apps with elevated API access. The permission model relies on the user explicitly granting access. If that grant is obtained under false pretenses or through a vulnerability chain, the attacker inherits the full privilege tier of the Shizuku server process.

---

### Scenario 2: Shizuku-Enabled App as a High-Value Target

**How it works:**

1. The attacker researches the device and identifies apps that have been granted Shizuku access (e.g., via the Shizuku manager UI, or by probing Binder service registrations).
2. The attacker finds a vulnerability in one of these apps — an exported component, an intent injection flaw, a content provider exposure, a WebView XSS, a plugin system with insufficient isolation.
3. By exploiting the app-level vulnerability, the attacker executes code within the context of a Shizuku-enabled app.
4. From there, the attacker uses the app's existing Shizuku connection to make privileged Binder calls.

**Impact:**

Amplified impact relative to a normal app compromise. A vulnerability in a non-privileged app gives the attacker that app's permissions. A vulnerability in a Shizuku-enabled app gives the attacker that app's permissions **plus** the Shizuku privilege tier — effectively escalating a low-severity app bug into a high-severity privilege escalation.

**Why it's possible:**

Shizuku-enabled apps are ordinary Android apps from the system's perspective. They have exported components, receive intents, expose content providers, and may contain bugs just like any other app. The Shizuku privilege does not magically make the app more secure — it makes exploits against the app more damaging. Any app that holds a Shizuku grant is a juicy target.

---

### Scenario 3: UserService Abuse

**How it works:**

Shizuku's UserService mechanism allows running custom Java/JNI code in a separate process under the Shizuku server's context. This is used legitimately for advanced features that require isolated execution. An attacker who can control or influence UserService behavior can:

- **Persistence:** Inject code that survives the lifecycle of the originating app. Even if the malicious app is uninstalled, UserService-hosted code may continue running if it has registered itself in a persistent manner.
- **Privilege escalation:** UserService code runs as the same UID as the Shizuku server (UID 2000 shell or UID 0 root depending on the privilege mode). This is higher than a normal app's UID.
- **Stealth:** Running in a separate process hides the malicious activity from the user. The Shizuku server process is a legitimate system-like process, and UserService code inside it is not visible in the app launcher or recent apps list.
- **Resource abuse:** A malicious UserService workload can consume CPU, memory, or I/O without the user's knowledge, since it runs outside any normal app sandbox visibility.

**Impact:**

A successful UserService abuse could result in long-term persistent access at elevated privileges, stealthy data exfiltration, or resource hijacking — all while appearing to be normal Shizuku server activity.

**Why it's possible:**

UserService is a feature designed for legitimate use cases, but like any plugin/code-loading mechanism, it expands the trusted code surface. If an attacker can influence what code UserService loads or executes, they gain a privileged execution environment.

---

### Scenario 4: Cross-App Attacks via Shizuku

**How it works:**

1. App A holds a Shizuku grant.
2. App A (or an attacker who has compromised App A) uses Shizuku's Binder APIs to interact with App B.
3. Depending on what Shizuku APIs are exposed and what permissions the shell/root UID holds, App A can:
   - Read App B's internal data directories (if file permissions allow at the shell UID).
   - Modify App B's components, intents, or shared preferences.
   - Call App B's exported components with forged parameters.
   - Change App B's granted permissions via package manager calls.

**Impact:**

One Shizuku-enabled app becomes a pivot point for attacking other apps on the device. This breaks the app sandbox model that Android relies on — two apps that should not be able to interact directly can be bridged through a privileged intermediary.

**Why it's possible:**

Shizuku provides access to system services (PackageManager, ActivityManager, etc.) that operate at a level above individual app sandboxes. When used to target other apps, these services can bypass normal inter-app isolation boundaries.

---

### Scenario 5: Wireless Debugging + Shizuku Network Exposure

**How it works:**

1. The device has Wireless Debugging enabled (Android 11+ feature allowing ADB connections over Wi-Fi).
2. Shizuku is running in ADB shell mode (UID 2000) — which it normally does when launched via Wireless Debugging.
3. An attacker on the same network (or able to reach the device's IP) connects via ADB over TCP.
4. Through the ADB connection, the attacker gains shell access.
5. With shell access, the attacker can interact with the Shizuku Binder service directly — at UID 2000 — without needing any app to be installed or any user grant to be obtained on the device.

**Impact:**

This is one of the highest-risk chains. It requires no user interaction on the device beyond having Wireless Debugging enabled. The attacker gains shell-level access to the Shizuku Binder service from the network, enabling remote execution of privileged operations. If Shizuku is running in root mode (Sui), the network-accessible attack surface becomes even more severe.

**Why it's possible:**

Wireless Debugging exposes an ADB interface over the network. Shizuku running under ADB inherits that context. The combination means the device is reachable and the privilege layer is active — two independently useful features that together form a dangerous superset. HackTricks explicitly flags this combination.

**Mitigating factors:**

- Wireless Debugging is off by default and must be explicitly enabled.
- Wireless Debugging requires pairing/approval on first connection (depending on Android version and settings).
- The device must be on a network the attacker can reach.
- Firewalls or network segmentation can reduce exposure.

---

## 3. UserService Exploitation Methodology

### What UserService Is

UserService is a Shizuku subsystem that allows running custom Java/JNI code in a process associated with the Shizuku server. It is designed for legitimate advanced use cases where a Shizuku client needs to execute code that requires the elevated UID but in an isolated process separate from both the client app and the core Shizuku server.

### Abuse Vectors

#### 3.1 Persistence

- **Mechanism:** If UserService can register a component that auto-starts with the Shizuku server, malicious code can survive the uninstall of the app that originally invoked it.
- **Detection signal:** A UserService process or registration that persists after the originating app is removed.
- **Audit approach:** Check whether any UserService-registered code has persistent entry points (broadcast receivers, services with `RECEIVER_EXPORTED` flags, scheduled jobs).

#### 3.2 Privilege Escalation

- **Mechanism:** UserService code inherits the Shizuku server's UID. In shell mode this is UID 2000; in Sui root mode this is UID 0.
- **Detection signal:** Processes running at elevated UIDs that are not part of the standard Android system image.
- **Audit approach:** Enumerate running processes by UID. Any non-system process at UID 2000 or 0 that is not obviously a system service warrants investigation.

#### 3.3 Stealth

- **Mechanism:** UserService code runs inside the Shizuku server process (or a closely associated process). It does not appear as a separate app in the launcher, recent apps, or standard app lists.
- **Detection signal:** Unusual CPU, memory, or network activity from the Shizuku server process that cannot be explained by normal Shizuku operations.
- **Audit approach:** Profile the Shizuku server process. Compare observed behavior against expected behavior for the Shizuku version in use.

#### 3.4 Resource Abuse

- **Mechanism:** Malicious UserService code can loop, consume memory, write large files, or make network connections — all under the Shizuku process identity.
- **Detection signal:** Device slowdown, battery drain, data usage, or storage consumption attributed to the Shizuku process.
- **Audit approach:** Monitor resource usage of the Shizuku server process over time. Look for anomalous spikes or sustained high usage.

### What to Look for When Auditing UserService Usage

1. **Which apps have invoked UserService?** Check Shizuku logs and the Shizuku manager UI for UserService start records.
2. **What code is UserService running?** If possible, inspect the loaded code or its behavior. Legitimate UserService usage should be traceable to a known app and purpose.
3. **Are there UserService instances that outlived their parent app?** A UserService that persists after the originating app is gone is suspicious.
4. **Is UserService used at all?** Many Shizuku users never touch UserService. Its mere presence is not inherently malicious, but an unexpected UserService is a signal worth investigating.
5. **What permissions does the UserService process hold?** At UID 2000 or 0, it holds significant capabilities. Verify that any UserService usage is justified by the app's function.

### Monitoring Signals

- Unexpected Shizuku server process behavior (CPU, memory, network).
- UserService start/stop events that do not correspond to known app activity.
- Binder service calls originating from UserService that do not match expected app behavior.
- Persistence mechanisms tied to UserService that survive app removal.

---

## 4. Malicious Client Crafting Notes

### Building a Shizuku Client

A Shizuku client is an Android app that uses the **Shizuku-API** library to connect to the Shizuku Binder service and make privileged API calls. The development path is straightforward for anyone familiar with Android development:

1. Add the Shizuku-API dependency to the app's `build.gradle`.
2. Request Shizuku access at runtime (the user must grant permission through the Shizuku manager UI).
3. Once granted, obtain a `IPrivilegedService` binder interface.
4. Call exposed APIs through the binder.

The Shizuku-API library handles the Binder connection plumbing. The attacker's app does not need to implement the server side — it just needs to be a client that the user (or an exploit chain) grants access to.

### What a Shizuku Client Can Do

Once connected, a Shizuku client can call system services through the elevated privilege context. Capabilities include but are not limited to:

- **System services:** Access to `ActivityManager`, `PackageManager`, `ConnectivityManager`, `Settings`, `PowerManager`, and other system services that are normally restricted.
- **Other app data:** Shell UID (2000) has broad read access to app data directories. Root UID (0) has unrestricted access to all app data.
- **Package management:** Install, uninstall, enable, disable, and modify permissions of other apps via `PackageManager` calls.
- **Protected APIs:** Call APIs that are signature-protected or privileged, which normal third-party apps cannot access.
- **Settings modification:** Read and write system settings, secure settings, and global settings.
- **Process and task manipulation:** Start activities, services, and broadcasts in other app contexts; manipulate running tasks.
- **File operations:** Read and write files across the filesystem at the privilege level of the Shizuku server.

### Root Mode vs. Shell Mode

| Aspect | Shell Mode (UID 2000) | Root Mode (UID 0 via Sui) |
|--------|----------------------|---------------------------|
| Access level | Shell-level system access | Full root access |
| App data access | Broad read access to many app directories; write access depends on file permissions | Unrestricted read/write to all app data |
| System modifications | Limited to what shell can do | Full system modification capability |
| Persistence potential | Moderate | High — can modify system partitions, install boot-time components |
| barrier to entry | Requires ADB or Wireless Debugging enabled | Requires Magisk + Sui module installed and approved |
| stealth | Moderate — shell process is visible in process listings | Lower — root process can be harder to attribute |

An attacker who can escalate from shell to root (e.g., by exploiting a kernel vulnerability, or by having the user install the Sui module) gains the full root-tier capability set.

### What Makes a Shizuku Client Effective

- **Low user suspicion:** A Shizuku client can be disguised as a legitimate utility app. The Shizuku permission grant screen is a normal part of the Shizuku UX, so users accustomed to Shizuku apps may grant access without carefully scrutinizing the requesting app.
- **Broad API surface:** The number of system APIs accessible through Shizuku is large. A single client can perform many different types of operations.
- **Separation of concerns:** The client app and the Shizuku server are separate processes. A compromised or uninstalled client does not necessarily take down the server, and the server's privileges persist as long as Shizuku is running.
- **Ecosystem familiarity:** With 980+ apps in the awesome-shizuku catalog, Shizuku grants are increasingly common. Users and even some security tooling may treat Shizuku access as routine rather than high-privilege.

### Limitations and Risks for an Attacker

- **User grant required (shell mode):** The user must explicitly approve the Shizuku access request. Social engineering or an exploit chain is needed to bypass this.
- **Wireless Debugging dependency (shell mode):** If Shizuku is running in ADB shell mode, Wireless Debugging (or USB debugging) must be active. If neither is enabled, Shizuku cannot start in shell mode.
- **Magisk dependency (root mode):** Root mode requires Magisk with the Sui module. On a non-rooted device, only shell mode is available.
- **Detectability:** Shizuku server process, Binder service registration, and privilege grants are visible to defenders who know where to look (see Section 5).
- **Attribution:** Operations performed through Shizuku are attributed to the Shizuku server process, not the client app. This can make forensic attribution harder but also means anomalies in the Shizuku process are detectable.

---

## 5. Defensive Audit Checklist

### 5.1 App-Level Checks

- [ ] **List all apps with Shizuku access.** Use the Shizuku manager app or query the system for apps that have been granted the `shizuku` permission. Record which apps hold grants.
- [ ] **Review the grant list.** For each app with Shizuku access, ask: Does this app need Shizuku? Is the app from a trusted developer? Has the app been updated recently? Is the app still installed and maintained?
- [ ] **Audit app permissions.** For each Shizuku-enabled app, review its full permission set. An app with Shizuku access plus excessive additional permissions ( Accessibility, overlay, notification access, location, microphone, camera ) represents a higher-risk combination.
- [ ] **Audit UserService usage.** Check whether any app is using UserService. If so, what app, what purpose, and is the usage still active? Any UserService usage by an unknown or unmaintained app is a red flag.
- [ ] **Audit Shizuku-enabled app vulnerabilities.** For apps that hold Shizuku grants, review their attack surface: exported components, intent filters, content providers, WebView configurations, plugin architectures, deep links. A bug in a Shizuku-enabled app is more valuable to an attacker.
- [ ] **Check for unexpected Shizuku clients.** If you see a Shizuku grant for an app you don't recognize or didn't intentionally grant, investigate. It may be a remnant of a previously installed app, or it may be unauthorized.
- [ ] **Review app update history.** An app that was recently updated may have changed its Shizuku usage patterns. An app that hasn't been updated in a long time may have unpatched vulnerabilities that are now more dangerous because of its Shizuku grant.

### 5.2 System-Level Checks

- [ ] **Determine the privilege tier.** Is Shizuku running in shell mode (UID 2000) or root mode (UID 0 via Sui)? Root mode dramatically increases the impact of any compromise. If root mode is not needed, consider using shell mode only.
- [ ] **Check Wireless Debugging status.** Is Wireless Debugging enabled? If yes, is it actively used? If not needed, disable it — especially when Shizuku is running. The combination of Wireless Debugging + Shizuku is a known high-risk chain.
- [ ] **Check how Shizuku access was granted.** Was access granted via ADB, via Wireless Debugging, via the Sui Magisk module, or via Device Owner provisioning (Dhizuku)? Each method has different security properties and different exposure. Understand which method is active and whether it's appropriate for your threat model.
- [ ] **Monitor Binder service registrations.** The Shizuku Binder service (`moe.shizuku.privileged.api`) is a registered system service. Monitor for unexpected Binder service registrations or modifications to the Shizuku service configuration.
- [ ] **Review Shizuku server logs if available.** Some Shizuku versions or configurations may produce logs of API calls, connection events, or UserService activity. Review these logs for anomalies: unexpected client connections, unusual API call patterns, UserService starts that don't match known app behavior.
- [ ] **Check for unexpected device behavior.** Symptoms that may indicate Shizuku-based abuse: unexplained battery drain, data usage, CPU activity, files appearing or disappearing, settings changing without user action, apps being installed/uninstalled/disabled, permissions changing on other apps.
- [ ] **Audit the Shizuku APK itself.** Verify the installed Shizuku APK is the authentic version from RikkaApps. Check the APK signature, version, and install source. A modified or repackaged Shizuku APK would be a critical compromise.
- [ ] **Check for other privilege escalation tools.** The presence of Shizuku alongside other privilege tools (Magisk, kernel exploit suites, other shell-access frameworks) may indicate a device that has been targeted for privilege escalation. Understand the full toolchain present.

### 5.3 Ecosystem Awareness

- [ ] **Track Shizuku security discussions.** Monitor the Shizuku GitHub repository issues, the Shizuku-API repository, and relevant security research for new findings, reported vulnerabilities, or changes to the security model.
- [ ] **Watch for new Shizuku-powered apps.** The awesome-shizuku catalog grows over time. New apps may introduce new attack surface or new misuse patterns. Periodically review new entries.
- [ ] **Monitor for new attack chains.** Security researchers periodically publish new chains involving Shizuku (e.g., the Accessibility + Wireless Debugging + Shizuku chain documented on HackTricks). Stay current on published research to understand emerging threats.
- [ ] **Understand the privilege model changes.** Shizuku's security model may evolve across versions. When updating Shizuku, review the changelog and any security-related notes to understand what changed in the privilege boundaries, permission checks, or access controls.

### 5.4 Incident Response Signals

If you suspect Shizuku is being abused:

1. **Identify the active Shizuku clients.** List all apps with Shizuku grants. Cross-reference with known/expected apps.
2. **Determine the privilege tier.** Shell or root? This sets the upper bound on what the abuse can do.
3. **Check UserService.** Is UserService running? What code? Who started it?
4. **Review Binder activity.** What APIs are being called through Shizuku? If you can capture or log this, it reveals what the abuse is doing.
5. **Check Wireless Debugging.** Was it enabled? Was it used to connect? Check ADB connection logs if available.
6. **Contain.** Revoke Shizuku access from suspicious apps. Disable Wireless Debugging if not needed. Stop Shizuku if the threat is active and you need to halt operations.
7. **Preserve evidence.** Before revoking or stopping, capture the state: running processes, grant list, logs, network connections. Forensic preservation helps with attribution and scope assessment.
8. **Remediate.** Uninstall malicious or compromised apps. Rotate any credentials or tokens that may have been exposed. If root mode was active and you suspect compromise, consider the device untrusted at the system level and evaluate whether a factory reset or device replacement is warranted.

---

## 6. Known Issues and Research Notes

### Security Model Awareness

The Shizuku team is aware of the privilege implications of their tool. The official documentation discusses the security model, the trust assumptions, and the user's responsibility in granting access. Shizuku is designed as a power-user tool, and its threat model assumes a knowledgeable user who understands the consequences of granting Shizuku access. This does not eliminate the risk of abuse, but it does mean some of the safety burden is placed on the user rather than on technical enforcement.

### Wireless Debugging Risks

Wireless Debugging is a known risk amplifier for Shizuku. It makes the ADB interface reachable over the network, and Shizuku running under ADB inherits that context. This combination is flagged in offensive security research (notably HackTricks) as a high-value target for attackers who can reach the device on the network.

### No Major Public CVEs

As of the research cutoff, no major public CVEs have been disclosed against the Shizuku server codebase. This is a snapshot observation, not a guarantee. The code's attack surface (Binder transactions, intent handling, permission checks, UserService, Wireless Debugging interaction, update mechanism) remains a valid target for future security research and responsible disclosure.

### Ecosystem Breadth as a Risk Multiplier

The awesome-shizuku catalog lists 980+ apps that integrate Shizuku. Each of those apps is a potential client — and each is an ordinary Android app that can have its own vulnerabilities. The ecosystem's size means there are many potential entry points. An attacker who finds a vulnerability in any one of these apps gains Shizuku-level privileges. Conversely, a defender must consider the entire set of installed Shizuku-enabled apps as part of the trusted computing base.

### Dhizuku and Alternative Privilege Models

Dhizuku uses Device Owner mode to gain elevated privileges, which is a different security model from the ADB/shell or Sui/root approaches. Device Owner mode is typically used in enterprise/MDM contexts and has its own set of trust assumptions and risks. It is a distinct vector that should be assessed on its own terms rather than lumped in with the ADB-based Shizuku modes.

---

*End of document.*

```
